Centre foreground is the flower-shaped Artscience Museum . Photo / Getty Images <p> With a fraction of our resources , Singapore 's enviable wealth is built around future planning and joined-up thinking . Meanwhile , NZ is culling its public sector , writes Anna Fifield . <p> In Singapore , bureaucrats wear a metaphorical badge of honour . They 're well regarded and well paid . Many are sent to the world 's best universitiesso they can apply the best thinking to running the government . Most civil servants stay for their entire careers . And it is widely appreciated that having a professional public service has been fundamental to Singapore 's economic success . <p> Yet Christopher Luxon , who clearly admires Singapore and has repeatedly called for New Zealand to be more like it , has promoted very few of the policies that have underpinned its astonishing transformation from struggling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in barely more than a generation . <p> Singapore has a highly capable civil service staffed by trusted technocrats who have been able to execute policy consistently over decades , and it continues to invest heavily in state capability . But in New Zealand , the political debate is increasingly focused on reducing rather than building the public service . <p> Luxon 's government has launched two assaults on the size of the civil service , most recently in the 2026-27 Budget , slashing thousands of jobs . Finance Minister Nicola Willis is targeting a core public service of 55,000 , requiring about 8700 job losses by the middle of 2029 , saying this would deliver savings of $2.4 billion over four years . <p> While National has avoided criticising public servants directly , Deputy Prime Minister and Act leader David Seymour , who complained about " bureaucrats creaming it " before he entered government , has n't been so restrained . He summed up the Budget announcement as " fewer departments , fewer bureaucrats , and the public service sucking up less taxpayer money " . <p> Gliding on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ top recruits , who are proud to be called bureaucrats . Photo / Getty Images <p> The approach is more reminiscent of Elon Musk 's Department of Government Efficiency than of Lee Kuan Yew , the visionary founder of modern Singapore . <p> " I have no doubt there is a lot of inefficiency in lots of governments , but you do n't solve the problem by setting arbitrary targets without understanding why the inefficiencies are there and where the inefficiencies are , " says Peter Ho , who spent 34 years in the Singaporean government , including as head of the civil service and permanent secretary of foreign affairs , national security and defence . <p> " All this looks good to the public but they are not thinking through the loss of capability : what you are saving versus what you are destroying . So I 'm very worried about cut , cut , cut without thinking about what they are actually cutting , " says Ho , who is now a senior advisor to the Centre for Strategic Futures in the Singaporean prime minister 's office . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ between Singapore and New Zealand . While Singapore 's population is only slightly bigger , at 6 million , it is ethnically and religiously diverse . And the city-state is n't blessed with New Zealand 's natural resources : it has a land mass less than half the size of Rakiura/Stewart Island . <p> Then there 's the fact it 's what political scientists call a " flawed democracy " with authoritarian tendencies . Since it gained independence from Malaysia in 1965 , Singapore has been ruled by the People 's Action Party , founded by Lee , a strongman who prioritised the collective good over the individual , often with an iron fist . Although there is technically an opposition , dissent is generally not well tolerated in Singapore . <p> Lee is credited with popularising the line , ' If you pay peanuts , you get monkeys . ' <p> Anna Fifield <p> That certainly helps to create consistency and ensure certainty , and analysts disagree about how much of Singapore 's success comes from bureaucratic excellence and how much is due to being run by the same @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ spoken admiringly on many occasions of the way Lee turned Singapore , a fledgling nation with a fractured society and no assets other than its port , into an economic powerhouse that now has one of the world 's highest incomes per capita : US$107,760 , more than double New Zealand 's US$52,020 , according to the International Monetary Fund . <p> " There 's no reason to come to Singapore unless we are so much better than our neighbours , " says one current civil servant , describing how the city-state is driven by its constant sense of vulnerability . " We do n't have sheep that get fatter or crops that grow while we sleep , " she said , asking to withhold her name so she could speak frankly . <p> Instead , Singapore created policies to become attractive to international investors and businesses : part of its allure is that it 's widely seen to be well administered and to offer policy and regulatory consistency . <p> " It 's understood that our civil service works , and as a public policy practitioner , we have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for everyone . We always want to expand our options for the future , " she said . <p> Singapore was ranked the world 's best in the most recent index of public administrations compiled by the Blavatnik School of Government at Oxford University , including for innovation and policy implementation . New Zealand also scored well , at sixth-equal . <p> Paul Goldsmith , Minister for the Public Service and Digitising Government , rejected comparisons of New Zealand 's public service with Singapore 's . <p> " New Zealanders expect a public service that grows smarter , not simply larger . Our government is acting , " he said by email , noting the public service expanded from about 47,000 to more than 65,000 people from 2017-23 , nearly twice the rate of growth in the overall labour force . Although some of it was necessary during the pandemic , it was unsustainable , he said , adding that back-office and support functions grew significantly faster than front-line service delivery roles . <p> " Looking to the future , we want to ensure we 're investing in a high-quality @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and financially sustainable over the long term , with a core focus on front-line delivery . " This would be achieved by reducing the number of government departments and increasing the use of AI . " Every dollar saved is a dollar that can be redirected toward improving productivity , supporting growth and improving New Zealand 's long-term economic resilience . " <h> Forging a path <p> None of Singapore 's success happened by accident . When Lee took full control of Singapore in 1965 , he believed it could not survive , let alone thrive , without having an exceptionally capable state . " We 're a tiny country . We do n't have much of a natural economic hinterland , and had to figure out ways to survive without one , " says Aaron Maniam , a senior civil servant who was founding head of the Centre for Strategic Futures and is now at Oxford 's Blavatnik School . " Achieving such survival meant and continues to mean thinking about the long term -- how to be as relevant as possible to the world . " <p> Lee @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In the public service , he created a system that was meritocratic , recruiting and promoting the smartest graduates rather than those with the right connections . To this day , 400-500 undergrads are sent each year to study in the best universities -- think Oxford , Yale -- on state scholarships , on the understanding they will bring their newfound knowledge home to Singapore for a set number of years , depending on where they go . ( Maniam compared Singapore and New Zealand 's digital systems as part of his doctorate at Oxford University . ) <p> When they enter government jobs , they go through training at the Civil Service College , and again when they get promotions to middle management . When they become directors , they do a six-week programme one participant likened to Harvard 's advanced executive education programmes . The courses cover governance and policy , leadership and organisational development . <p> Countries with existential threats tend to be most interested in foresight and scenario planning . <p> Anna Fifield <p> Those identified as potential leaders often take year-long breaks after 9-12 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ top university , or an attachment to a company or multilateral organisation . <p> " The idea is partly to let people renew their energy , but also to upskill and renew their international networks , " says Maniam , who worked at the Civil Service College . <p> And civil servants are well paid . Lee is credited with popularising the line , " If you pay peanuts , you get monkeys . " <p> In the beginning , the generous salaries were paid to try to make sure there was no corruption in the system and build a clean public service . Now , it 's to make it less likely that top-notch people will go to work at places like McKinsey or Deloitte . Public sector salaries are benchmarked against the private sector to ensure they do n't get too out of whack . <p> All of this is , of course , very expensive and hardly likely to be adopted here , especially considering Singapore 's civil service is already much bigger than New Zealand 's -- it 's about 1.47% of the population compared with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lee , who died in 2015 , was also known to ruthlessly reorganise ineffective or inefficient agencies . <p> The Singaporean system faces other criticisms . It 's considered very hierarchical and top-down , accused of being elitist , recruiting from only the top universities , and a demanding place to work . Because it 's a quasi-authoritarian system , there 's little -- if any -- consultation with the public . For all these factors , analysts have pointed out it 's hard to replicate elsewhere . <p> But Singaporean civil servants describe the pride they take in their jobs . When I referred to Maniam as a bureaucrat then quickly apologised for using a pejorative term , he said : " It 's a compliment for us . " Another saw positive connotations in the term " Byzantine bureaucracy " : the administrative system continued to operate in Byzantium even as emperors came and went . <h> Futures planning <p> The key factor that sets the Singaporean government apart from many others , though , is its use of long-term planning , especially a practice known as " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ opportunities , technologies and social changes . It is designed not to predict the future but to prepare for multiple possible futures . <p> In 1995 , the prime minister 's office set up the " scenario planning office " to map out potential risks and plausible responses . The need was proven by the unexpected events of the late 90s and early 2000s , especially the Asian financial crisis and the Sars outbreak . One of its earliest exercises produced two visions of Singapore 's future . The first was called " Hotel Singapore " and anticipated a possibility of a commercially successful , cosmopolitan Singapore , but one that produced a generation who felt no strong ties to the city-state , instead treating it like a hotel . It was the Singaporean version of New Zealand 's " brain drain " . The second was called " A Home Divided " and imagined a polarised Singapore where people identified with their ethnic , religious or educational groups but did not feel an overarching sense of belonging to the nation of Singapore . <p> This had a dramatic impact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economic growth were the main drivers , " says Ho . " But this awakened in the Singaporean government an awareness that they should pay attention to , and spend money on social development . It 's not productive but it has big-picture ramifications . " <p> In Singapore , the whole system is invested in this -- from the prime minister down to junior officers -- and the planning office has since been renamed the Centre for Strategic Futures . " We are always questioning our assumptions and making no assumptions that we have got everything right , " says Ho . " We ca n't just write papers , we must close the loop . National scenarios are all linked to the budget and implementation is very important . Otherwise it 's just an academic exercise . " <h> Our existential crisis <p> Countries with existential threats tend to be most interested in foresight and scenario planning , Maniam says . He cites Finland and Estonia , which live in the shadow of Russia , and the low-lying Netherlands , which faces imminent threats from climate change . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ becoming irrelevant has powered much of its planning . <p> New Zealand , with its geographic vulnerability laid bare by the supply chain disruptions of Covid-19 and now with the Strait of Hormuz crisis , is also experiencing a kind of existential angst as the last stop on the planet . <p> Some planning work does take place here . The government introduced a requirement in 2020 for ministries to produce Long-Term Insights Briefings every three years , and a raft of them were published last year . There 's the Climate Change Commission and Infrastructure Commission , and the Reserve Bank does stress testing in the banking industry , while the Treasury has its Living Standards Framework . But there is nothing like the kind of joined-up , whole-of-government thinking that Singapore 's Centre for Strategic Futures does , according to several former officials and analysts . <p> The Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet ( DPMC ) did set up a small strategy unit before the Covid crisis , but it has been wound down -- even though Luxon , a former CEO well known for using corporate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and was reportedly a fan . <p> Survival continues to mean thinking about the long term -- how to be as relevant as possible to the world . <p> Aaron Maniam <p> Although the strategy unit is gone , the principles of strategic foresight and futures thinking remain integral to the DPMC , a spokesperson said . Other units of government use long-term planning to guide strategy and resilience to national risks " and support future-focused policy development across the public sector " , the spokesperson said . The department was currently focused on improved results in health , education , law and order , employment , housing and the environment during the next four years . <h> Short-term thinking <p> Our system also comes with the upheavals of democracy Singapore does n't need to worry about . " I do think a key challenge in New Zealand is a structural factor -- you have elections every three years , " says Maniam . " By itself , this time horizon can make it difficult to do any long-term planning . If there is any seriousness about doing more long-term @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ how the long-term planning process is de-linked from the electoral cycle , so there are incentives for long-term plans to be made and actually put in place . " <p> This is a sentiment shared by Jonathan Boston , emeritus professor of public policy at Victoria University of Wellington , who has been encouraging governments for years to build up capabilities for long-term thinking and anticipatory governance . <p> " One of the critical reasons we have governments is to enable a society to identify the risks it faces and to mitigate and manage those risks , as well as exploit opportunities , " says Boston . " From a risk management point of view , there are all manner of risks that individuals and subnational entities do n't have the ability to manage : natural disasters like floods , geopolitical risks like external threats , and technological risks that can undermine social cohesion . " <p> The current government is " short-term-ist , " he says . He cites recent changes to the Public Service Act , which , when enacted in 2020 , said the public service " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ facilitates active citizenship " . Amendments enacted this month remove both these purposes from the act . <p> " The focus now is on the role of the public service in pursuing the priorities and needs of the current government , with less emphasis on its stewardship and guardianship role that has , for generations , underpinned the durability and longevity of the state , " says Boston . " A core role of the state is to be doing horizon scanning , looking decades if not generations into the future . It should be looking for the big opportunities on the horizon that we can seek to harness for the betterment of the people . " <p> In this world of extraordinary and unprecedented change , New Zealand needs to ask itself the Singaporean refrain : Why does the world need us ? <p> Anna Fifield travelled to Singapore on a grant from the Asia New Zealand Foundation 's Asia Media Centre . 
@@138121397 <p> When prompted to write corporate sustainability reports , ChatGPT glossed over sustainability issues and produced language ranked high in metrics for greenwashing . Photo : Getty Images <p> Expert opinion from University of Auckland - Waipapa Taumata Rau <p> When ChatGPT first hit universities , two academics at the University of Auckland 's Business School decided they might as well incorporate it into coursework . Why not get students to experiment with generating a corporate report ? <p> Ruth Dimes , who led the business master 's programme at the time , and Charl de Villiers , a professor of accounting , instructed postgraduate students to produce a typical corporate sustainability report using generative AI . Then , they mixed up the AI reports with real ones and had students blindly assess them . <h> read more <p> Dimes and de Villiers found that ChatGPT glossed over sustainability issues and produced language ranked high in metrics for greenwashing . Moreover , students preferred the fake reports over real ones . " They were very persuaded by the language in in ChatGPT , " says Dimes . <p> These students @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ really informed group of students , " says Dimes . " They were studying accounting , they were studying sustainability reporting , they 'd been through all of the things around what companies try to do and what greenwashing is . " <p> Still , they preferred reports that made a company 's social and environmental disclosures sound great , even if the company 's performance on those fronts was average . <h> Where greenwashing seeps in <p> Most companies include information about their own environmental and social impact in their reporting -- as well as how environmental and societal factors might affect them . " So , how the changes in the natural environment might influence the company 's ability to make money in the future , " says de Villiers . " And then the influence of the company on the fabric of society and on the natural environment . " <p> In some jurisdictions , it 's compulsory to make these disclosures -- the European Union , for instance , requires both these perspectives to be included in corporate reporting . <p> The University of Auckland Business @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ produce the reports using AI . Photo : Supplied <p> Want to stay across stories like this ? Start your day with Newsroom 's top stories and analysis in your inbox . <p> Disclosing this information can lead to greater trust from investors , making it easier for a company to raise money , but research finds that companies do n't always tell the full story about their environmental impacts . " The critics of these companies say , ' You 're greenwashing -- you 're emphasising the best points and you 're ignoring the bad points , ' " says de Villiers . <p> Sustainability is increasingly important in investors ' decision-making , write Dimes and de Villiers in their research . Environmentally focused investments are big money -- Bloomberg estimated they will exceed $40 trillion by 2030 . Deception introduced by greenwashing is concerning from both an economic and environmental perspective . <h> How the researchers tested greenwashing <p> Dimes designed an assignment where students used ChatGPT to create a CEO statement to shareholders -- a summary that would normally sit alongside a sustainability report -- for a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ use a hypothetical Hawkes Bay forestry company as the setting . Forestry was interesting , says de Villiers , because of its connection to climate change , and because it has upsides and downsides on environmental and social impacts . <p> " We prompted the students to write this report using AI and then to re-prompt and re-prompt to improve it -- and to show us what they did , " says de Villiers . " But they took the first output they got . They did n't do much more ... maybe they read it and they went , ' Well , this is much better than I would have done ' . " <p> Dimes took the reports , randomised them , added three real corporate reports , and gave them to other students to rate on which they found most believable . <p> " When we asked students to rank them in terms of a bunch of different things like ' credibility ' and ' would you invest in this firm ' , et cetera , they liked the ones that ChatGPT came up with better , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ students did n't carry any real-world responsibility for the results of their choices , says Dimes -- a factor which can affect a study 's results . " If students make a decision like , ' I trust this company ' or ' I do n't trust this company ' , there 's no consequences to what they were doing , whereas someone in a real job who 's investing , it 's different . " <p> But these students are about to enter the workforce , likely going into roles where they will do just that , says Dimes . " I mean , that 's exactly the job . So , there 's not that much difference between a master 's student specialising in sustainability reporting and a sustainability analyst who would have that background . " <h> Why greenwashing rises to the top <p> Next , Dimes and de Villiers put the ChatGPT reports through a greenwashing detection tool . " And yes , they were quite greenwashy , " says de Villiers . <p> In other words , they were too good to be true , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ones had real issues that they were not able to gloss over -- they had to admit to certain issues , whereas the made-up ones did n't have that problem . " <p> Notably , Dimes and de Villiers found that reports that had been edited by students were n't any better than the ones straight out of ChatGPT . More prompts or more tinkering with the output text did n't change the level of greenwashing . <p> This suggests it 's hard to mitigate the impact of AI use with human intervention . AI 's overly confident tone , people 's inability to detect AI use , and people 's willingness to believe it are " worrying factors " , according to Dimes and de Villiers . <p> " Auditing and assurance of it is still quite kind of nascent compared to financial reporting , which is subject to a lot of regulation , " she says . " There needs to be some kind of control or regulation or guidance because there 's still a lot of choice about what you say or what you choose to omit @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ choice . " <p> Another solution ? " Companies might find their investors want hard evidence to back up the sustainability stories they tell in their reports , " says de Villiers . Otherwise , the very real risk is shareholders and future investors viewing sustainability reporting as mere AI fabrication . <p> The world is facing unprecedented environmental challenges . @@138136506 <h> How Taiwan is balancing between American and Chinese visions of energy dominance <p> U.S. President Donald Trump 's declaration of a national energy emergency on his first day back in office framed fossil fuel production as a geopolitical weapon . " Energy dominance " -- flooding global markets with American oil and liquefied natural gas ( LNG ) -- would reassert American power , undercut China 's clean-technology leverage and discipline allies into dependence . Eighteen months on , the doctrine is revealing some of its contradictions , and nowhere more acutely than in Taiwan . <p> The numbers behind assertions of U.S. dominance are real . Boosted by the shale revolution initiated in 2005 , oil and gas production has reached record highs , with over 13.6 million barrels of oil per day in 2025 . U.S. LNG exports already commanded roughly one-third of the global market before the Hormuz crisis and the EU could depend on the U.S. for 80 per cent of its LNG imports by 2028 . <p> These are in part compliance purchases , not simply market ones . East Asian and European countries @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ alternatives and to protect their fraying U.S. security umbrella . They are also seeking to avoid higher tariffs and address shortfalls resulting from Russian and U.S. military aggression , not because the economics have been compelling . <h> Taiwan 's energy predicament <p> If China symbolizes the electro-state and the U.S. the petrostate , most other states occupy an uncomfortable middle : dependent on imported fossil fuels , scrambling to build renewable capacity and watching the two giants ' rivalry with mounting anxiety . <p> What makes Taiwan 's position distinctive is not just this triple bind , but the fact that it sits at the intersection of structural forces reshaping global energy . Its semiconductors are the physical backbone of the clean transition , essential to AI infrastructure , smart grids , EV controllers and solar inverters . <p> Ships move through the Taiwan Strait as seen from the 68-nautical-mile scenic spot , the closest point in mainland China to the island of Taiwan , in Pingtan in southeastern China 's Fujian Province . ( AP Photo/Ng Han Guan ) . <p> There is , however , a harder @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Energy dominance , as a doctrine , mistakes the instrument for the goal . Control over fossil fuel flows is not the same as strategic resilience , as the Hormuz disruption demonstrated . Countries responding to that shock are not concluding they need more oil ; they are concluding they need less exposure to it , and that U.S. behaviour is having painful economic costs . <p> For the United States , it means recognizing that fossil fuel supremacy is not a durable form of power ; and that for middle-income states caught between the two superpowers , it increasingly resembles a costly and clumsy protection racket , rather than a respectful strategic partnership advancing long-term energy security and climate liveability . <p> This article was co-authored by Suzanne Duroy , a full-time journalist based in Taiwan . <p> Your access to our unique and original content is free , and always has been . Learn more here . <p> Remember we welcome robust , respectful and insightful debate . We do n't welcome abusive or defamatory comments and will de-register those repeatedly making such comments . Our current comment policy is here . <h> 3 Comments <p> ' In contrast , the U.S. increasingly passes for an insecure incumbent petro-state reliant on its military might , fossil fuel endowment , and a disregard for international law and climate change , to reassert an outdated form of energy dominance . ' <p> A dinosaur hegemony , on the way out . Meanwhile , smoking remains the biggest preventable cause of cancer in Aotearoa . <p> There was the bold and abrupt Equal Pay Amendment Act last year , which introduced stricter and higher thresholds for raising pay equity claims and obliterated the 33 pay equity claims from female-dominated workforces that were active at the time . The Wharf St lights , Beacon Wharf , Masonic Park , Waterfront Park North , the Waterfront Playground and Tunks Reserve are among the sites to light up . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ youth fundraising event . <p> This year 's campaign encourages participants to disconnect from screens while raising money for children in Solomon Islands . <p> Across the country , young people are taking on a wide range of challenges , including endurance activities , community service projects and creative fundraising efforts aimed at highlighting the realities faced by vulnerable communities . <p> World Vision national director TJ Grant said he was continually impressed by the creativity and commitment shown by participants . <p> " It 's inspiring to see so many young people embracing creative challenges and giving up screen time for a short time to support vulnerable children in Solomon Islands , where nearly half of children suffer from chronic malnutrition and climate change is making it increasingly difficult for families to put food on the table , " he said . <p> Funds will go towards providing families in Solomon Islands with seeds , tools and training to grow more crops . Photo / World Vision <p> Grant said community support , including the lighting of landmarks , reflected the event 's long-standing place in New Zealand . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of New Zealand 's cultural heritage and it 's an honour that so many community organisations and monuments show their support for this campaign , New Zealand young people , and the children we are raising funds to help . " <p> Other landmarks lighting up orange around the country include Auckland 's Sky Tower , Spark Arena and Eden Park , Christchurch 's Botanic Gardens and art gallery , and Wellington 's Michael Fowler Centre . <p> Funds raised this year will go towards providing families in Solomon Islands with seeds , tools and training to grow more resilient crops , helping ensure children have consistent access to nutritious food . <p> Young people can take part individually or through schools , churches or community groups , with registrations open online .

@@138180756 <p> Comment : The thing about methane is that it offers a way to slow global heating . Making substantial emissions reductions of this gas would take a miracle but we need miracles because we are like lemmings at the cliff edge . <p> Our own cliff requires little more than a higher sea wall compared with , say , Pakistan where climate change has made monsoon rains extreme . In 2020 , floods were so severe that at least 1500 people died and 16 million children were affected . In the Himalayan and Karakoram ranges , where normally four glaciers melt each summer , in recent years 16 have shed their water into Pakistan -- which contributes 1 percent to global greenhouse gas emissions . <p> So Pakistan and many other parts of the Third World need this miracle more than we in industrialised countries who have inadvertently caused these disasters . Radically reducing methane emissions would be like reducing the flow of a hot tap to a trickle . The inexorable rise in world temperatures would continue , but at a dwindle. <h> read more <p> Methane differs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ powerful for a very short time . Climate science puts it at between 80 and 90 times more efficient at warming the planet than carbon dioxide but only for the 25 to 30 years it hangs around the world . It then dissipates into CO2 and continues as a warming blanket for centuries . <p> It 's a big ask and the prospects are not good but we have to look at them . <p> Methane is ranked as the second most significant greenhouse gas , after carbon dioxide . As measured by scientists at the Global Carbon Project , based at Stanford University in the US , these emissions have increased by 20 percent in just the last two decades . <p> Methane , says Rob Jackson , the project 's chair , is a climate menace the world is ignoring . <p> He 's not referring to methane released by thawing tundra and from wetlands and degrading tropical forests . He 's concern about methane produced by human activities . The thing about methane from this source , he says , is that unlike other fossil fuels , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ need it for wind vanes or solar panels or to make batteries for EVs -- all of which require , in one way or another , coal or oil . Methane contributes nothing to the petrochemical industries which produce those plastics we use daily . There are other ways to heat houses and cook food . <p> Want to stay across stories like this ? Start your day with Newsroom 's top stories and analysis in your inbox . <p> More than half of all methane emissions from human activities are reckoned to come from agriculture . Rice is the problem but if we reduced the 1.5 billion cows that graze the planet , the methane from rice fields would be insignificant . <p> Climate science is hard to live with -- so hard that the majority of us live as if it does n't exist . What the science says about methane is especially hard for a country whose economy is based on the products of nine million methane-exhaling ruminants . <p> New Zealand is a signatory to the Global Methane Pledge , established by the Biden administration and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are committed to reduce methane emissions by at least 30 percent below 2020 levels by 2030 . But it 's non-binding so we wo n't be castigated because the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade admits we 'll be pushing it to make even 10 percent by 2030 . <p> Former Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment Simon Upton foresaw the size of our problem . In his 2019 report Farms , Forests and Fossil Fuels , he advised dealing with methane according to its different sources . Fossil methane refers to what we know as natural gas , which is 90 percent methane and comes out of the ground . Biogenic methane is the term for that which comes out of the gut of ruminant animals . <p> This is the so-called split gas solution . <p> The important thing about biogenic methane for us is that it makes up 55 percent of our total greenhouse gas emissions , according to a 2024 report by Simon Upton ( now into his second term as parliamentary commissioner for the environment ) . <p> We ca n't not address this problem . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from the New York Review of Books on June 11 , 2026 : <p> " ... New Zealand -- a Pledge signatory and one of the world 's biggest livestock and dairy producers -- cut its mid-century targets for methane by half after lobbying from the agriculture industry . " <p> The announcement by the Government of the $51 million AgriZeroNZ public-private partnership to extend research into methane inhibitors as feed additives , including seaweed and the chemical known as 3-NOP , will no doubt be closely watched . But reducing emissions by 20-30 percent will do nothing to reduce the nitrogen in our waterways or the tropical deforestation associated with the international beef industry . <p> A long-term view would see that in a climate-changed world land will be a priority and hectare-for-food value will have us eating more plants . The Stanford scientists estimate that beef provides less than 1 percent of calories globally but accounts for 5 percent of greenhouse gas emissions . Dairy and meat are non-essentials for health but have become prestigious symbols of affluence in non-western cultures where economies thrived for centuries on plant-based @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ agriculture underpins our way of life . No political party dares look beyond next year 's returns on milk powder and beef , especially after recent record profits . Why does climate adaptation not allocate some of that $51m to support those farmers who are adapting their practices by reducing cow numbers , going organic or venturing into horticulture ? <p> How severe a storm will it take ? Climate science tells us we 're effecting in a couple of centuries changes equivalent in magnitude to those that occurred over millennia . Paleontologists have enabled us to conjure images of those distant eras but we stretch our imaginations with dread to picture what the world could be like in as short a time as another century . <p> Yes , curtailing methane would be a good thing . But it is important to distinguish between biogenic methand and fossil methane . The former come from cattle etc eating grass that has taken up carbon dioxide during photosynthesis . When methane decays in order a decade it returns to carbon dioxide : fully circular . Not so with fossil methane . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of fossil methane from mining and industrial sites , oil and gas production , and leaky wells . Those should be easily fixed with incentives and should be the first target . Australia 's fugitive methane is comparable to all of NZ 's biogenic methane ! China is a major contributor . We need to cut emissions and that includes carbon dioxide in NZ . <p> Cut herd sizes by 10% to 15% now . It will barely make a dent in the profits , since any additional cow on the overcrowded irrigated fertilised gravel plain needs so many inputs that they cancel out the revenue from it . <p> Beef farming in particular is one of the most energy-inefficient and destructive industries ever invented . It has a round-trip efficiency of around 3% ( fossil fuel cars and green hydrogen are around 30% efficient , batteries about 85% to 90% ) . If it was a new technology , it would n't even get past the experimental lab stage . We give it a free pass , though , because it has thousands of years of history , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ land has to be farmed , no matter what . That 's called maladaptive behaviours , and they are one of the root causes of the mess we 've made . <p> Bravo Pat . Some people claim that biogenic methane " does n't matter " because it 's formed from CO2 via photosynthesis and gut microbes , and only in the atmosphere for a short time before reverting to CO2 . Hence ' no additional warming ' . You do n't need an understanding of chemical kinetics and an ability to solve differential equations to see that this is total garbage . During its time in the atmosphere , methane is a very potent GHG . If ruminant farming was replaced by other forms of farming , most of that methane would n't be there at all -- it would still be CO2 with a much lower GWP . With a number of potential tipping points approaching , we need to LOWER all greenhouse gas emissions as soon as possible . Methane emissions from farmed mammals are now massively greater than they were in pre-industrial times . There are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( a ) those who get what 's happening and ask ' what more can I do ' ; and ( b ) those who deflect blame and try to get away with doing as little as they can . It 's certainly a bit rich for townies to blame hard-working farmers while busily planning their next long-haul flying holiday or climbing into their unnecessary but oh-so-sexy gleaming new double-cab ute . On the other hand unless we ALL face reality and do our bit the future will get very grim very fast . As adverse weather events escalate , farmers will be the first to take a hit .

At the time , the prime minister indicated he only became aware of the document through the media . <p> Christopher Luxon has since announced an investigation by the Department of Internal Affairs and the Ombudsman has a investigation underway . <p> The complaint - which RNZ has seen - raised " serious concerns about procedural defects and substantive inconsistencies with the rule of law , the separation of powers , and New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said that included the right of access to a court , minority rights under Article 27 of the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights , and the climate due diligence obligations articulated by the International Court of Justice . <p> The government announced in May it would amend climate laws , preventing findings of liability in torts - a type of civil case where one person or entity claims another has caused them harm . <p> Justice Minister Paul Goldsmith said it would apply to current and future cases - stopping a landmark case against Fonterra and five other major emitters in its tracks . <p> In 2024 , Smith was granted permission by the Supreme Court to sue Fonterra and other major dairy and fossil fuel companies . <p> Goldsmith said the law change was to deal with " uncertainty in business confidence " created by the case . <p> Justice Minister Paul Goldsmith . <p> RNZ / Mark Papalii <p> Lawyers for Climate Action acting executive director Laura MacKay said the tort prohibition was " not just bad climate policy " , but it " strikes at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the right of New Zealanders to have their day in court . <p> " The government 's own officials advised that there is no evidence the proceeding was having any measurable impact on business confidence , and that the constitutional and access-to-justice costs outweigh any certainty a bar might provide . <p> " Despite that , the government is pressing ahead . This is compounded by information recently revealed about corporate lobbying to the Prime Minister 's Office for exactly this law change . " <p> Writing to the Special Rapporteur , Elisa Morgera , they asked her to intervene before the legislation was introduced to Parliament . <p> The 125-page complaint outlined the background to the case , the Smith v Fonterra legislation , and the proposed government amendments . It also discussed the procedural and substantive effects . <p> Within the procedural effects , the complaint stated the " unknown extent of engagement with the corporate defendants " of the case . <p> Lawyers for Climate Action acting executive director Laura MacKay . <p> Supplied / Lawyers for Climate Action <p> It stated there was " inconsistent information @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the defendant companies in Smith v Fonterra " . <p> It referenced the documents released by court order , and the revelation these were handed to a senior Beehive staffer as well as sent to his private email address . <p> " The briefing notes explained the " rationale for proceeding with the introduction of legislation to prevent " Smith v Fonterra from continuing . <p> " They explained that legislative intervention " is necessary and appropriate " , as it would " resolve the uncertainty and risks posed by private law claims like Mr Smith 's " . " <p> The complainants noted they were not contending " cause and effect " but that it was " very concerning when information is withheld from the public record , and wide-reaching legislation very similar to that advocated for in that information is implemented without further consultation with other impacted parties " . <p> " It is in stark contrast to the recent report of the Special Rapporteur , " The imperative of defossilizing our economies " , where two of the recommendations are for States to prohibit lobbying by the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ defenders ' protection and access to justice and effective remedies " . " <p> The complainants said the government 's proposed legislation was the " latest in a sustained pattern of climate regression in New Zealand since October 2023 " . <p> They respectfully requested the Special Rapporteur issue a " communication to the New Zealand Government prior to the Tort Prohibition Bill being introduced to the House of Representatives " .

@@138217263 <p> A University of Auckland legal scholar is warning that Aotearoa risks repeating mistakes exposed by the Canterbury earthquakes and Cyclone Gabrielle unless stronger legal protections are put in place to safeguard people 's right to housing during disasters . Dr Maude Loutsch says as extreme weather events and natural disasters become more frequent and severe , New Zealand ... <p> A University of Auckland legal scholar is warning that Aotearoa risks repeating mistakes exposed by the Canterbury earthquakes and Cyclone Gabrielle unless stronger legal protections are put in place to safeguard people 's right to housing during disasters . <p> Dr Maude Loutsch says as extreme weather events and natural disasters become more frequent and severe , New Zealand must rethink how it approaches disaster recovery and housing security . <p> Her research argues for a human rights-based approach that recognises housing as more than simply buildings and infrastructure . Instead , housing should be viewed as a fundamental human right connected to dignity , culture , community , wellbeing and identity . <p> The warning comes as many communities across the country continue to grapple with the long-term @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Loutsch 's analysis examines disaster responses in Tonga , Japan and New Zealand , including the aftermath of the Canterbury earthquakes and Cyclone Gabrielle . The research identifies significant gaps between legal commitments and the reality experienced by affected communities . <p> One of the key findings is that existing laws and disaster response mechanisms are fragmented , creating uncertainty around responsibilities and limiting accountability when people lose access to safe and adequate housing . <p> The research highlights a number of shortcomings in current approaches , including a narrow understanding of housing rights , insufficient attention to human rights standards during recovery efforts , limited participation by affected communities in decision-making processes , and inadequate avenues for redress when people believe they have been treated unfairly . <p> For Maori communities , housing security is often deeply connected to whenua , whakapapa , whanau and cultural identity . When disasters force people from their homes , the impacts can extend well beyond physical damage and affect social , cultural and emotional wellbeing . <p> Loutsch argues that governments must take a more active role in defining , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ occur rather than responding after the fact . <p> Advocates say stronger protections would help ensure that recovery efforts place people and communities at the centre of decision-making and that vulnerable groups are not disproportionately affected during times of crisis . <p> The research also raises broader questions about how Aotearoa prepares for the growing impacts of climate change , with scientists warning that severe weather events are likely to become more frequent in coming decades . <p> As communities continue to recover from recent disasters , the study serves as a reminder that rebuilding homes is only part of the challenge . Ensuring people can maintain connections to their communities , culture and support networks is equally important . <p> With climate-related emergencies becoming an increasing reality , experts say strengthening housing rights now could help prevent future disasters from creating deeper social and economic inequalities .

@@138229949 <h> The virtue of working together <p> For those who cynically suggest that our politicians can not work together on anything , Parliament this week offered evidence to the contrary in the form of the government 's response to the National Infrastructure Plan . <p> The foreword to the response , as one might expect , was penned by Minister for Infrastructure Chris Bishop . However , following that were equally as supportive statements by Labour 's infrastructure spokesman Kieran McAnulty and his Green Party counterpart Julie Anne Genter . <p> While the government and Opposition are not quite in lock-step on this issue they are , more or less , singing from the same song sheet . <p> Chris Bishop . PHOTO : ODT FILES <p> They could hardly not , because the response was to a solid and eminently sensible report issued by the Infrastructure Commission in February . It called for a four-step approach to developing infrastructure : planning for what New Zealand can afford , looking after existing infrastructure properly , prioritising the right projects and making it easier to build better . <p> Most importantly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and building infrastructure , one which placed the country 's needs first and foremost rather than whatever the politically expedient need of the day was . <p> It appears to have got its wish . <p> Mr McAnulty said that while Labour and National would never agree on every minor detail , that was no reason to withhold support from the direction of travel . <p> ' ' The prize here is bigger than any single policy . This is a framework durable enough to survive changes of government , so the sector can plan , invest , train and hire with confidence . ' ' <p> Ms Genter 's support was more measured -- the Greens support long-term investment in infrastructure , but predominantly with the eye towards decarbonisation . <p> ' ' Climate change can not be treated as political football , ' ' she said . <p> ' ' It is a reality that is already impacting existing infrastructure , and will shape our near and long-term energy , transport and water infrastructure needs . ' ' <p> Mr Bishop called that ' ' the kind of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' ' ; he said the government fully supported 13 of the commission 's recommendations and the other three in principle . <p> Among other things , the government will develop a 30-year National Infrastructure Plan ; continue to support National Infrastructure Funding and Financing Limited ( an investment portal for overseas infrastructure investment ) , improve education and health infrastructure and invest more in electrification . <p> The plan means more investment in roads , but it also means more spending on risk mitigation work such as stopbanks and other flood defences . It will also mean bringing water infrastructure up to scratch , although the expense of that alone will make that noble ambition problematic . <p> Other recommendations are already , broadly , under way . Parliament is considering Resource Management Act reform to improve the consenting framework -- something that Labour has given some measure of cautious support for , albeit it still wants to see what devils may lie in the details . <p> The government is also in the throes of establishing MCERT -- the Ministry of Cities , Environment , Regions , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ministries of the environment and transport , . <p> Much of the management and oversight involved in making the plan a reality is anticipated to rest on its shoulders , and remains to be seen if it is up to such heavy lifting . <p> Something which must also be considered is the needs of provincial and rural New Zealand , and how to balance that against the big cities . Of the funded and unfunded projects in the National Infrastructure Pipeline , 40% of them are in Auckland -- and probably fair enough too , given a third of New Zealanders live there . <p> However , a quarter of Kiwis call the South Island home , and just 11.6% of pipeline funding is destined for it . Otago and Southland combined receive 4.3% . <p> Yes , things are being built in the South -- the new Dunedin hospital is the most obvious example of that -- but healthcare is not the only vital infrastructure component needed . <p> New Zealand spends a lot on infrastructure -- around 5.8% of GDP -- but the OECD ranks the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it is now up to all of us to do the hard work required to turn ambition into delivery . The multi-party commitment to the infrastructure plan is a good start . 
@@138229952 <p> In less than four years since OpenAI launched ChatGPT , AI technology has shifted from theoretical and technical conversations among computer scientists in the digital tech industry and universities , to a principal theme of general discussion . <p> The dramatic emergence of AI within human societies is already having , and will continue to have , a significant impact across all sectors of human societies . <p> Advocates praise the greater efficiency and productivity , and the resulting benefits that AI will bestow upon humanity . However , the speed at which AI technologies are developing and the rapid deployment of their use have curbed essential ethical reflection . <p> In a famed scene from the 1999 movie , The Matrix , Agent Smith , the representation of the AI programme , pins the head of the protagonist , Neo , to train tracks in front of an upcoming train and states : ' ' You hear that , Mr Anderson ? That is the sound of inevitability . It is the sound of your death . Goodbye , Mr Anderson . ' ' <p> Two recently released @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Humanitas , the first encyclical letter issued by Pope Leo XIV , discusses the ethical implications of the emergence and adoption of AI technologies for human dignity and societal wellbeing . <p> The encyclical pushes back against the narrative of inevitability . While acknowledging that ' ' AI can be a valuable tool ' ' , Leo XIV argues that ' ' the use of AI is never a purely technical matter ' ' but that its development and use reaches to the core of what it means to be human , touching on our understanding of human ' ' rights , opportunities , status and freedom ' ' . <p> Rather than accept a discourse of technological determinism , the encyclical employs two Biblical images to illustrate the choice humanity faces . <p> In option one , willingly or unwillingly , we participate in the building of another Tower of Babel : constructing an economy managed by a wealthy elite who control the technology and reap the financial benefits , thus creating a society that ' ' sacrifices human dignity for efficiency ' ' . <p> Option two requires @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , in which ' ' the idolatry of profit sacrifices the weak ' ' . Here , Leo XIV takes his cue from the Book of Nehemiah , an account of Jewish exiles returning to rebuild the walls of Jerusalem destroyed 150 years earlier by the Babylonian empire . <p> For Leo XIV , while ' ' technology has the power to heal , connect , educate and protect ' ' , this is only possible when we build together for the common good . <p> Noting that technological innovations , like AI , can ' ' foster participation and justice or exacerbate inequality , control and exclusion ' ' , Leo XIV contends that our use of such technologies must be evaluated according to a key question : do these technologies ' ' truly help individuals and peoples to become more humane and fraternal , while respecting our common home and future generations ? ' ' <p> The second report , the Global Justice Report , authored by the World Inequity Lab led by French economist Thomas Piketty , confronts the challenges of climate change and global inequality . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a blueprint of an alternative future . It is not , the authors contend , inevitable that we face a grim future of increasing global inequality and diminished planetary habitability . <p> Climate change and increasing societal and global inequality , the report insists , are not separate but inextricably connected and solvable issues . The report argues that to change the global trajectory and construct a future of human wellbeing for all , and planet habitability requires three key shifts within our political economies . <p> Firstly , what is required is the fast decarbonisation of our economies . That is , a rapid uptake of renewable , sustainable energy sources . <p> Secondly , economies need to shift from a focus on ' ' growth ' ' and increasing ' ' prosperity ' ' to that of ' ' sufficiency ' ' . <p> This shift towards ' ' sufficiency ' ' is not merely a mental shift , but an achievable practical reality implemented through three key steps : ( 1 ) halving our average working time , from 2100 hours a year to 1000 hours ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ consumption patterns , specifically our dietary choices ; and ( 3 ) a refocusing of the economy towards low-consumption activities , specifically education and health . <p> Thirdly , the report posits that this rapid decarbonisation and a shift towards a low-consumption economy of sufficiency is only financially and politically possible if there is a drastic reduction in inequality of income , wealth and power , both within societies and between countries . <p> Addressing inequality through our economic and political decisions is a ' ' necessary condition ' ' for the construction of a decarbonised , low-consumption economy , built not to benefit a wealthy elite , but the common good of humanity . <p> Both reports make clear that we should not accept the two prevailing discourses of inevitability : AI as the salvific technology that solves all our problems , or , the opposite , an apocalyptic narrative , where out-of-control AI , climate realities and inequality lead to humanity 's inescapable demise . <p> The reports remind us that despite emerging technologies and contemporary challenges of our own making , humanity , made not as a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have dignity and agency . <p> Collectively , we can choose the future we pursue . Do we desire a future of equality and ecological sustainability which seeks to preserve and enhance the dignity and wellbeing of all of humanity ? <p> Or do we continue down a path in which humanity functions machine-like in a technocratic economy that exacerbates inequality and injustice , and leads to planetary destruction and human diminishment ? <p> Will we participate in constructing a new Tower of Babel or join together to build a world for the common good that enhances human dignity ? <p> * Dr Andrew Shepherd is a senior lecturer in Theology and Public Issues in the Theology programme , University of Otago .

@@138249237 <h> #tangaroa : Maui Dolphin Fight Puts Bottom Trawling in the Spotlight <p> A conservation leader is calling for urgent action to protect the critically threatened Maui dolphin , warning that destructive fishing practices continue to put one of Aotearoa 's rarest marine mammals at risk . Natalie Jessup , General Manager of Tangaro Tuia te Ora , says the fight to save the Maui dolphin must include stronger protections for the coastal ... <p> A conservation leader is calling for urgent action to protect the critically threatened Maui dolphin , warning that destructive fishing practices continue to put one of Aotearoa 's rarest marine mammals at risk . <p> Natalie Jessup , General Manager of Tangaro Tuia te Ora , says the fight to save the Maui dolphin must include stronger protections for the coastal habitats the species relies on for survival . <p> Maui dolphins are found only in Aotearoa and remain under serious threat from human activity , including fishing-related risks , pollution , disease , habitat degradation and the wider impacts of climate change . <p> Bottom trawling is a major concern for conservation advocates because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ affect the food chains and habitats that vulnerable species depend on . While set nets are widely recognised as the biggest fishing-related threat to Maui and Hector 's dolphins , trawl nets have also caught dolphins and remain part of wider calls for stronger marine protection . <p> Tangaro Tuia te Ora has been advocating for an end to bottom trawling in sensitive marine areas , including habitats used by Maui and Hector 's dolphins . The organisation says protecting these areas is essential if Aotearoa is serious about preventing extinction . <p> Policy changes being called for include stronger no-trawl zones , greater protection across critical dolphin habitats , tighter regulation of fishing methods , better monitoring and enforcement , and long-term marine planning that places biodiversity and kaitiakitanga at the centre . <p> Jessup 's work also focuses on building community support for conservation . Tangaro Tuia te Ora has been working with communities , iwi , environmental partners and advocates to raise awareness , support habitat protection and encourage collective action for endangered species . <p> For many Maori communities , the protection of Maui dolphins is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ taiao for future generations . Conservation leaders say saving the species will require both strong policy and active community leadership . <p> Everyday New Zealanders are also being encouraged to play a role . People can support petitions calling for stronger marine protections , back organisations working to protect endangered species , choose seafood carefully , reduce pollution entering waterways and vote for policies that prioritise ocean health . <p> Advocates say the survival of the Maui dolphin is not just an environmental issue . It is a test of whether Aotearoa is prepared to act before a taonga species disappears . <p> With numbers critically low , conservation groups say the window for action is narrowing . The message from Tangaro Tuia te Ora is clear : protecting the Maui dolphin means protecting its home . 
@@138261201 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> A selection of New Zealand philanthropists . Photo / Composite <p> Businessman and philanthropist Andrew Barnes has donated $22.7 million to research at Cambridge University after his partner , Charlotte Lockhart , was diagnosed with stage four breast cancer . Jane Phare looks at philanthropy in New Zealand , who our biggest donors are and what drives them to give . For the full list , scrollto the bottom of the story . <p> It was an example of fundraising on steroids : a young teenager in remission from cancer stood in front of 250 heavy-hitting philanthropists and rich listers in Auckland last month and helped raise $3.65 million in one night . <p> Hand-picked guests listened to Avie Skye , 14 , tell her heartrending story at a chic Cirque du Soleil-style event with one end goal -- to raise millions of dollars desperately needed to help save the lives of Kiwi children with cancer . <p> There @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Starship Foundation 's Star Gala fundraiser and seated at tables costing $15,000 for 10 guests . They knew they were there to give money . The question was , how much ? <p> Avie told them why she was lucky to be alive , saved by revolutionary cellular therapy available in the US but not available to New Zealand children . <p> Three years ago , the teenager was diagnosed with an aggressive form of leukaemia while her American family were based in New Zealand . But it was advanced cell and gene therapy that eventually saved her life . <p> The Bensch family , who donated more than $1m to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ New Zealand to ask guests to dig deep to boost the fund . <p> The Bensch family , from left , Hadyn and Avie Skye with their parents , Jenn and Freddy Bensch , at last month 's Star Gala . Photo / Alisha Lovrich <p> Zuru 's Nick Mowbray and Jaimee Lupton could n't attend but bought a $15,000 table for colleagues and pre-pledged $240,000 for one of the " auction " items , the cryopreservation programme enabling children with cancer to have ovarian or testicular tissue removed before chemotherapy to give them a chance for a family later in life . <h> From cake stalls to black-tie functions <p> It is an example of how philanthropy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ quiz nights are still the mainstays of fundraising at a community level . But at the top , philanthropy is well-organised , targeted and accountable . <p> The big givers do n't particularly need to go home having won a weekend away on Waiheke . Instead , they 're happy to bid on the charity 's wish list . <p> Gretchen Hawkesby says near the end of 12 years spent fundraising for Starship , people would bid for a bed in the hospital 's paediatric intensive care unit ( PICU ) rather than a prize for themselves . <p> The same applied at Auckland Grammar School , where her three sons attended . Guests would enthusiastically bid for a bunch of life jackets for the rowing team rather than a spa treatment . <h> The personal connection <p> Robyn Scott , acting chief executive for Philanthropy New Zealand , says philanthropy is increasingly seen as more than just a financial transaction . Donors give to causes that have " touched their head and their heart " . <p> Mowbray and Lupton are an example of that . They 're constantly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a year . <p> But after Lupton faced difficulty conceiving and then lost a baby daughter at 24 weeks , they 've pledged $20m over the next five years to fund Gingernut 's Angels , which gives grants for IVF fertility treatment to Kiwis struggling to conceive . Nearly $2m in grants has already been distributed , resulting in live births and pregnancies . <p> Zuru 's Jaimee Lupton and Nick Mowbray have pledged $10m to fund IVF rounds for people trying to conceive . <p> Lupton says the mother of the first Gingernut baby , Billie , born last year , sent a video of her daughter 's first steps . <p> " Being able to have those real-life touch points with these families that we have had the honour of helping is , I mean , there 's nothing greater than that . " <p> Mowbray , who has battled Crohn 's disease for 15 years , throws his financial support behind Crohn 's and Colitis NZ with the ultimate goal of finding a cure . <h> Money well spent <p> Those who have money , plenty of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be spent . Their view is that although most charities have well-meaning intentions , not all of them are well run and they do n't want their money wasted . <p> Top-level philanthropists employ staff who not only scrutinise requests from charities but make sure the organisation is running efficiently and offer professional help if it is not . <p> Billionaire businessman Hart is low-key about the family 's philanthropy . News of lump-sum donations appears here and there : $10m to the University of Otago to establish a dental teaching and treatment facility in South Auckland ; $6.5m to upgrade Starship 's PICU ; and massive aid relief , after an eruption-driven tsunami smashed into Tonga in 2022 , in the form of 30 fishing boats , eight tractors and containers of food . <p> But in fact the philanthropy is ongoing . The Hart family employ two people fulltime to help decide which charities , appeals and organisations get what . Hawkesby says they do a " truckload " of due diligence on each of the charities , give feedback and advice , and report back to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ make a decision . " <p> Those decisions tend to be in areas of children 's health , poverty , education , mental health and immediate needs -- like buying food for families at Christmas time . <h> Kiwis donated $3.8 billion <p> New Zealanders are known to be generous by nature in terms of supporting charities and crowdfunding pages , and volunteering . <p> Kiwis also dig deep when disaster strikes , donating $8.3m to a Givealittle page for disaster relief after Cyclone Gabrielle . Before that , they donated $92m to the Christchurch Earthquake Appeal Trust in 2011 and , after the Christchurch mosque shootings in 2019 , donated more than $21m . <p> But times , and available cash , are tougher now with everyday donors faced with a cost-of-living squeeze , high fuel prices and 29,200 registered charities competing for desperately needed philanthropic dollars . <p> John Green , who now chairs the Hugh Green Foundation , a multimillion-dollar legacy left by his father , thinks some wealthy New Zealanders could and should do more to help those less fortunate . <p> John Green , chairman @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his father . <p> " They 're entitled to do what they want with it ( money ) but the way the world and New Zealand 's going , relying on governments to get people out of trouble is not going to happen , is it ? " <p> But he also thinks New Zealanders , by their nature , are n't skilled at asking for help or money . He thinks if wealthy people are approached the right way , they will invariably help . <p> Green is a member of the exclusive golf club , Tara Iti at Mangawhai , and admits that , before joining , he was expecting members to be out of touch and unaware of the need around them . <p> " But they actually do ( understand ) . " <p> He shoulder-tapped members and others to donate to a cause this year and found no shortage of those prepared to help . <p> Scott says that Inland Revenue ( IRD ) could do more to encourage donations by making the claiming of the 33% tax credit easier . She and others in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tax credit donations to $100,000 a year , meaning a maximum of $33,333 can be claimed . <p> Robyn Scott , acting chief executive of Philanthropy New Zealand . <p> She and others in the sector want to simplify the system after research by Community Foundations of Aotearoa showed that less than half of Kiwis claim the credit , largely because they say the reclaim process is too hard . <h> What will happen to the Baby Boomer wealth ? <p> Millennials and Gen Z are expected to benefit from $1.2 trillion in wealth from Baby Boomer parents in the next two decades , some of which will be left in " willanthropy " to charities and research . <p> Last year , 17 philanthropists left large amounts of money to the University of Auckland in their wills . In total , the university received $51.5m from 2788 donors , $28.2m of which went to medical research . <p> But younger generations , inheriting millions from their Boomer parents , may not have the same benevolent attitude towards philanthropy and , if they do , might want to support different @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Osnos , author of The Haves and the Have-Yachts , studies the uber-rich and says there 's increasing evidence that the new generation of wealth , particularly in areas like Silicon Valley , do not have the same view of philanthropy . <p> New Yorker columnist and author of The Haves and the Have-Yachts , Evan Osnos . Photo / Pete Marovich <p> " They are much more suspicious of charitable obligations that have , in their minds , a liberal bent . " <p> That sense of old-style " noblesse oblige " has diminished , he says . <p> Osnos uses Tesla and SpaceX billionaire Elon Musk as an example . Musk tops the Forbes 2026 Billionaires List , with a worth of $1.4 trillion , but does n't feature in the Forbes list of America 's 25 biggest givers . Instead , he considers Tesla qualifies as philanthropy because it helps to contain climate change . <p> " These guys at the very top have a hugely influential effect because younger tycoons who are coming up , they watch what the biggest players in the field are doing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not as important as it was to a previous generation of tycoons like Warren Buffett . " <h> Giving while living <p> Hawkesby says increasingly philanthropists embrace the " giving while living " concept , rather than leaving lump-sum donations in their wills after they die . <p> An astonishing example of this is MacKenzie Scott , the ex-wife of Amazon founder Jeff Bezos , who has so far given away more than $44 million of her fortune since 2019 , including $12.3m donated last year. ? <p> MacKenzie Scott is intent on giving away her fortune in her lifetime . @@138261320 <h> Questions linger over potential plans for South Dunedin <p> An unconfirmed plan that could require the loss of one in four South Dunedin homes has been met with a noisy political backlash that may not reflect public sentiment . Mary Williams hit the streets and talked to residents . <p> Carolyn Sime , her children and Boogie the pug dog have lived on Surrey St in a stately 1907 four-bed villa since 2016 , the year after the big flood . <p> The Forbury street is notorious for water lapping at doorsteps during heavy rainfall and Ms Sime quickly guessed why a journalist was on hers . <p> ' ' You 're here about the flooding . Our house has never flooded . I guess it would n't have stood for 100 years if it did . It is just a wee bit higher , I think . ' ' <p> She laughed that during one flood event ' ' wood chips floated off the corner of my garden ' ' but that had been the extent of it . <p> Carolyn Sime ( holding Boogie ) . PHOTO @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ common and understandably defensive response around here ; there can be comfort in history and South Dunedin has weathered horrible flooding , so far . <p> However , residents who spoke to the ODT this week were aware the future looked more watery and tough decisions were needed to keep the low-lying area 's nose above rising waves . <p> The council-led South Dunedin Future Programme -- which has led multiple rounds of community engagement and commissioned research on risks and solutions -- had communicated tough news this week . <p> Three versions of a proposed plan -- one of which needs to be agreed by the community and council to get started -- all require large-scale council purchase of homes to make way for flood solutions . <p> There are 6500 homes in the area , and between 1100 and 1700 homes would need to be acquired , South Dunedin Future Programme manager Jonathan Rowe says . <p> That means more than one in six homes , possibly more than one in four , would go . <p> They would be replaced by a mix of solutions : raised @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ store water and more pumps and pipes to remove water . <p> There would also be a sea wall built around the harbour . <p> South Dunedin is known as much for its community spirit as its social deprivation , and social leaders are always clamouring for more social housing . Some new builds , possibly of medium density , are also indicated on the plans -- but it remains unclear how many people , or who , they could house . <p> The reasons for the plan are well known . South Dunedin sits on reclaimed wetland precariously protected by eroding dunes to the south and an only-slightly raised harbour to the north . The shallow groundwater can not drain naturally , so more intense storms and rising sea levels are expected to cause more flooding -- unless a plan is decided . <p> Mr Rowe said house acquisition , using powers in the Public Works Act , would happen within the first 25 years of any accepted plan , but there were uncertainties . <p> ' ' A lot of the acquisitions would need to occur in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but it depends ... If the impacts of climate change are faster or slower we may need to accelerate or decelerate . <p> ' ' Similarly , if resources did not allow for acquisition at a fast rate we might have to slow it down for that reason . ' ' <p> Mr Rowe stressed that cracking on with sustaining South Dunedin -- at a cost of up to $2.4bn over 100 years with significant disruption and change -- was better value for local and central government investment than leaving things to possibly go horribly wrong , with houses uninsurable and uninhabitable . <p> There were plenty of uncertainties , however , and a danger that climate change happens faster than expected , funds are released too slowly to combat impacts and there are potentially worsening climate change impacts beyond 2125 . <p> Council also had to consider funding alongside ' ' the city 's other issues ' ' , Mr Rowe said . <p> An obvious question was raised earlier this week : ' ' Can the council name streets where houses will be bought ? The presentation indicated the area could be replaced with extensive recreation grounds . <p> There were additional , smaller areas of purple in parts of central South Dunedin , although they were a little harder to pinpoint and varied between iterations of the plan . <p> Back in Surrey St , Ms Sime , an ICU nurse , had not heard about the plans to lose houses .
