@@138261366 <h> Tread softly with South Dunedin <p> Home is where the heart is , we 're told . Our house is a very , very , very fine house . Our house in the middle of the street . <p> Whether you choose a proverb often attributed to Pliny the Elder or song lyrics belonging to either Crosby , Stills , Nash &amp; Young or Madness respectively , you get the picture of the importance of home . <p> In this mind-bending , wearying and worrying world , everyone deserves a safe haven where they can retreat , regroup and dream with family and friends , away from the many pettifogging bureaucracies of the world beyond the front door . <p> Any threat to that security , that future , hits extremely hard . The fact the purveyors of that bureaucracy can still , legally , take away that sanctuary , your place , seems unjust . <p> The 2015 flooding in South Dunedin . PHOTO : STEPHEN JAQUIERY <p> Compulsory purchase is arguably a homeowner 's worst nightmare . <p> That unfortunate reality has uncomfortably come a step closer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the latest details of the South Dunedin Future programme , run jointly by the Dunedin City Council and the Otago Regional Council . <p> The long-term programme was set up after the highly damaging floods of June 2015 with the intention of coming up with a masterplan to deal with flooding in the area by 2100 , at a cost of billions of dollars . <p> In March last year , the programme released seven possible adaptation futures for the area , which is home for 14,000 residents and more than 1000 businesses . Since then , following consultation and community feedback , that has been whittled down to the three options laid out this week . <p> Few could argue that South Dunedin can carry on the way it is . The malign influence of climate change , with rising sea-levels and more frequent severe rainstorms , is going to have an increasing impact on the suburbs on the flat . Some Caversham residents also have to endure another quite separate issue , with raw sewage flowing down their streets after heavy rain , due to inadequate wastewater systems @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would n't be fair to leave so many residents in a huge state of anxiety every time a decent easterly or southeasterly begins moving across the Otago coast . Neither can councils turn a blind eye to the likely problems of insurers refusing to cover the homes of thousands because the risk of flooding damage is too high . <p> The three options outlined by programme manager Jonathan Rowe are ' ' protect ' ' , at an estimated cost of $2.098 billion and involving the demolition of 1190 buildings ; ' ' restore ' ' , costing $1.629b with 1162 buildings demolished ; and ' ' reshape ' ' , the most transformative of the three , at a cost of $2.448b , with 1738 demolitions and 44.5ha of land raised . <p> Councillors at both councils are scheduled to discuss the options next week and then there will be extensive public consultation , expected to run from July 1 to August 9 . <p> An adaptation masterplan will then go before the DCC and ORC for consideration in the middle of next year . <p> Since the narrowed-down @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ South Dunedin residents saying they have been blindsided by the likelihood of property acquisitions . Dunedin city councillor Jo Galer also said residents had discovered in a ' ' callous way ' ' what might happen to their homes from the maps released and from media rather than the council . <p> Upset residents have a point that , if more specific information is being released , the council should ensure it operates as transparently as possible and the affected communities are told first . <p> However , this is not the first time the details of possible property acquisitions have been outlined . Depending on which option is pursued , between 1100 and 1700 out of 6500 South Dunedin properties could be affected . <p> Unfortunately , narrowing the alternatives down to three is bound to spook the horses . There is understandable nervousness around the uncertainties for those most likely to be affected , which is why the councils have to proceed with as much caution as possible and not put a foot wrong in trying to take the community along too . <p> Proper consultation begins in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plan to succeed . <p> Communication with those affected should always remain at the top of the priority list . <p> As W B Yeats said in He Wishes for the Cloths of Heaven : ' ' Tread softly because you tread on my dreams . ' ' 
@@138261675 <p> Caption:Hannah Hughes says she feared she would die when she got caught in a massive avalanche.Photo credit:Supplied/Hannah Hughes <p> When an avalanche came barrelling down a mountain towards Hannah Hughes in Norway there was barely time to think , just a moment to brace for impact like a rugby tackle on ice . <p> The 34-year-old New Zealander was about to be caught by an unstoppable wall of snow that would send her tumbling uncontrollably almost 200 metres downhill while backcountry skiing in Trollfjorden . <p> " I thought if I plant my feet really firmly in my skis and shoulder into the avalanche like a rugby tackle , maybe I 'll be able to keep my feet firm on the ground , " she said . <p> " When the first chunks of snow hit me I thought , ' yeah , I 've got this ' , then the main body of the avalanche hit me like a freight train . I got taken off my feet instantly and was under the snow . " <p> Hughes thought she was going to die . <p> " I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it was so heavy . I could feel my left leg twisting up beside me more and more . There was so much pressure on my leg , I was honestly waiting for a bone to snap , " she said . <p> " With the weight of the snow on top of me it was dark . It was completely black . The snow sets like concrete around you . " <p> Another burst of movement sent her further down the mountain , head downhill , face up . <p> " As it stopped there was lightness around my face . I could see that it was n't pitch black any more . My arm was free enough that I could clear my face and I was above the snow . My whole body up to my chest was buried but my face was free , " she said . <h> Skiers survive in ' absolute miracle ' <p> Hughes was on the mountain with nine other skiers , two SeilNorge guides and a skipper from one of the boats on 4 March as part of a bucket @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by her backpack and stuck in the snow , Hughes eventually managed to sit up to survey the chaotic scene . <p> While some skiers were above the snow , including her partner , three needed help to free themselves , one person had to be dug out from well over a metre deep and another was still missing . <p> " That was when it really hit me , the emotion of it . I remember sitting on the side of the hill watching everyone trying to find this person and silent crying to myself , " she said . <p> In an " absolute miracle " , Hughes said the missing skier was soon pulled out unconscious and came to quickly . <p> " We 're so insanely lucky that nobody died , that 's a testament to the people who were there that day who rescued them . It really is pure luck that they were buried for 15 to 20 minutes and they 're both alive , " she said . <p> Guessing she had a fractured and dislocated left ankle , Hughes hooked her arm @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to safety by a frozen lake . <p> She said the group did not receive a response to radio calls for help because of the remote location but were relieved to eventually hear a helicopter approaching . <p> " Luckily one of the skippers on the boats was a retired police officer and managed through all the crackling to catch the word ' avalanche ' . He was smart enough to realise something was wrong and had sailed out into the strait and called search and rescue , " she said . <p> Hughes was winched to safety with the rest of the group and flown to hospital on the mainland where she had surgery for her broken ankle and was diagnosed with torn anterior cruciate and medial collateral ligaments . <p> Back home and on crutches in Whistler , Canada , Hughes began to realise just how close she had come to death . <p> " It was n't until we got home that I realised just how much the trauma of the avalanche had shaken me . It took me a while to call it a near-death experience @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ thought that was it . The fact that everyone survived is an absolute miracle , " she said . <p> " It hit me all at once , how much I was going to miss out on . I had a bunch of ski plans and hut trips booked for the rest of winter and I had to cancel . I honestly cried for a week straight when I got home , just devastation at how much I was going to miss out on . <p> " It feels like a bad nightmare that I 'd like to wake up from . " <p> The aftermath after the wall of snow hit the group . <p> Supplied/Hannah Hughes <h> Avalanche forecast warns of considerable danger <p> Months later , Hughes is angry that SeilNorge 's guides ventured up the mountain in conditions she believes were unsafe and regrets failing to read the avalanche forecast that warned people to " stay out of all avalanche terrain and run-out zones " . <p> She had been spooked by the deaths of nine people in an avalanche near Lake Tahoe two weeks before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ guides . <p> " The whole reason you pay guides is because they know the snow pack , the terrain and everything better than you , " Hughes said . <p> " To have put our trust in guides and have this be the outcome is pretty devastating . It 's not the ticket to safety that I thought it was . " <p> The group planned to ski a line called TM7 , tour over another mountain to an alpine sauna , then ski down to the boat in the dark . <p> Skinning or uphill skiing , Hughes said she raised concerns with a guide about small , loose wet avalanches as they moved through three-to-four centimetres of rain-soaked storm snow . <p> She also noticed debris from a size two avalanche in the last 24 hours . <p> Hughes said she spoke up three times about signs of instability on the mountain , including repeatedly slipping on the skin track and falling over , but her concerns were dismissed . <p> She was shocked when one of the guides later told her he was only expecting size @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I 'm injured and the effect it 's going to have on my life for the next couple of years is pretty massive . @@138311998 <p> In the second of a two-part series on the Supreme Court , Lady Deborah Chambers , KC , warns of judicial activism and lack of clarity . <p> Now for the bad . <p> Courts exist to decide cases impartially and according to law . They uphold the rule of law , but they are not designed as legislativebodies . We should therefore expect close legal , media and political attention to Supreme Court decisions and appointments . Parliament also deserves scrutiny when it passes vaguely worded laws that effectively require the court to fill in policy gaps . <p> One of the strongest critiques has come from Emeritus Professor Peter Watts , KC . In his New Zealand Law Review article , Watts argues that the Supreme Court 's 2022 decision in Ellis v R represents a revolutionary departure from New Zealand 's constitutional foundations rather than a gradual evolution of the law . <p> The Ellis case concerned the convictions of Peter Ellis , a gay Pakeha man found guilty in highly controversial circumstances of sexually abusing numerous children at the Christchurch Civic Creche . Notably , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Before Ellis , the role of tikanga Maori ( Maori customary law and values ) in the common law was limited and reasonably settled . It was recognised mainly in cases involving customary Maori property rights or matters closely connected to Maori affairs , particularly certain family law issues . <p> In Ellis , the court went much further . It effectively declared that tikanga is potentially relevant to any question of common law or statutory interpretation that might come before the courts . <p> Watts contends that the court has departed from the statutory foundations of the courts ' jurisdiction ( the starting point of which is English common law ) , both in its substance and methodology . Other criticism is that the Supreme Court has overturned long-standing rules for the recognition of custom without providing a clear new framework to replace them . <p> Watts argues that the Ellis decision has cast a cloud of uncertainty over the entire existing body of case law and statute law , creating real problems for access to justice and equality before the law , since only those expert @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ requires in many situations . <p> Watts does not oppose tikanga having a place in New Zealand law . His concern is the method : a sweeping change effected by judicial decision rather than through the democratic process . At the time , the Government had already asked the Law Commission to review the role of tikanga in New Zealand law . <p> Rather than wait for that considered public process , the court chose to decide the broader issue in a case that was really only about whether an appeal could continue after the appellant 's death -- a question that did not require any ruling on tikanga , which was not even argued by the parties . <p> Ellis is not an isolated example . Critics point to a pattern . In Fitzgerald v R , the Court read down clear statutory wording on third-strike sentencing . In Smith v Fonterra , it allowed novel climate tort claims to proceed . Parliament has since signalled legislation to limit such claims . <p> Parliamentary sovereignty is a fundamental part of our constitutional system . Parliament has undone Supreme Court @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ step with what it intended . It is perfectly entitled to do so because it is the elected body that makes statute law and can change it at any time , including by overriding court decisions . <p> These cases are frequently cited as the court advancing particular policy outcomes on Maori rights and tikanga , climate change , and a more cautious approach to criminal sentencing -- outcomes that align with a broadly liberal , insulated Wellington-Beltway centric worldview . Unlike politicians , judges are not tested by an opposition . On the contrary , they are usually surrounded by people who agree with them . <p> A long-standing complaint concerns how the court writes its judgments . Multiple separate judgments , even when they reach the same result , often make it extremely difficult for practitioners and lower-court judges to know what the law now is . <p> A striking example is Scott v Williams , the leading case on economic disparity compensation between spouses in relationship property proceedings . Economic disparity compensation is intended to recognise the financial disadvantage one spouse often suffers when they have taken @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Parliament left the method of calculating that compensation vague , leaving the courts to fill in the detail . <p> In Scott v Williams , five separate judgments left the law so impenetrable that ordinary New Zealanders , such as women like Mrs Te Wiki from Pukekohe , after a traditional marriage can not get a compensation payment because they can not afford to engage forensic accountants and specialist lawyers simply to work out how the economic disparity provisions in the Property ( Relationships ) Act apply . <p> The uncertainty has also consumed a large amount of judge time in the lower courts . It is no good constantly talking about the importance of access to justice when the top court 's own decisions have the opposite effect . The foot soldiers of the justice system -- the lawyers and judges who have to apply the law day to day -- and the taxpayer deserve greater precision . <p> The same problem appears in criminal law . A clear example is Brooker v Police 2007 NZSC 30 , where the Supreme Court delivered multiple separate judgments on the meaning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Those judgments made the legal test complex and difficult to apply in practice . Police officers and lower-court judges have since found it hard to know with confidence what crosses the line in protest or expressive conduct cases . <p> This has left the people who actually have to apply the law on the ground , the " troops " , if you like , struggling . It creates a real practical difficulty for police officers scratching their heads when , for example , someone burns a New Zealand flag during a protest , and police have to try to decide whether that should be treated as offensive behaviour or not . Frankly , who knows ? <p> The court should make much greater use of single or plurality judgments -- one agreed set of reasons from the judges who reach the same outcome , with short postscripts if needed , and without preventing succinctly expressed genuine dissent . A real attempt should take place after the hearing to try to produce more coherent majority reasons on the understanding that this is what New Zealanders want and need from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ appearing , more deliberate attempts at post-hearing agreement among the judges could help produce clearer majority reasons . <p> The combined effect of difficult-to-reconcile judgments and decisions that appear to expand judicial power into policy territory is reduced predictability . Litigants and lawyers find it genuinely difficult to forecast how the court will approach novel issues . Uncertainty at the top affects everyone below it . <p> Even a valuable institution , and Part 1 showed the Supreme Court has real strengths , benefits from rigorous scrutiny . The good does not cancel out the bad . Legitimate questions remain about the clarity of the court 's judgments and the proper limits of its role . Without sensible but vigilant criticism , judicial activism risks becoming normalised . When that happens , we risk moving from the rule of law toward something closer to the rule of judges . <p> Catch up on the debates that dominated the week by signing up to our Opinion newsletter -- a weekly round-up of our best commentary . 
@@138312028 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> Cloud competitive : The OVHcloud data centre in northern France boasts AI-driven features to reduce power and water requirements . Photo / Getty Images <p> Artificial intelligence is here to stay -- so how can we minimise the risks without sacrificing the benefits ? This is what we should be asking , rather than speculating about how soon we 'll all be turned into paper clips by an evil AI agent blindly following instructions . <p> The AI conversationhas been derailed by hyperbole . Discussion swings between the poles of boom -- life will become a universal paradise run by machines -- and doom : all hail , mighty paperclip overlords . <p> Although the doomsday scenario has imaginative appeal , not one of the sober and sane experts interviewed for this article gave the paperclipalypse possibility credit . But they did have plenty of scary stuff to say about things that are happening right now . <p> Before going much further , it would help to have an agreed-upon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . As California-based Jason Howell , whose " AI Inside " podcast is focused on the issues confronting us , admits , " There 's no real definition that means the same thing to everybody . Am I really describing artificial intelligence ? Am I really describing an algorithm we just throw into the bucket of AI ? Am I actually describing machine learning ? " <p> At a basic level , your calculator is AI , performing an activity much better than your brain can . Predictive text on your phone is AI , doing something that looks like thinking ahead for you . <p> A rough definition , covering most of what we think of when we talk about AI , is " any form of computing that performs a function otherwise performed by the human brain " . <p> Presented with this definition , a chatbot responded in a way that was both patronising and defensive , describing it as " close to the core idea of AI , but with a few problems " . <p> One thing AI is not -- and is not likely @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is AGI , or artificial general intelligence , which is what we mean when we talk about thinking machines , and held out as the ultimate goal by tech companies . The so-far-insurmountable problem with that is that such a machine would have to have some sort of consciousness . And since we do n't understand how our own consciousness works , we 're not really in a position to invent a machine that has it . <p> Theresa Z ? ger , head of the AI and society lab at the Alexander von Humboldt Institute for Internet and Society in Berlin , is dismissive . " I find it fascinating that computer scientists talk about consciousness without ever considering reading any of the hundreds of books that have been written in biology and philosophy and other sciences about what consciousness actually might be , and are still claiming that this will emerge by just making language models better , " she says . " Yes , you will be better at simulating it . And if that is what you mean by AGI , fine . This will probably @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ simulation . " <p> " AGI is a gimmick , " says Virginia Dignum , author of The AI Paradox and professor of responsible artificial intelligence at Ume ? University , Sweden . <p> She and other experts argue tech companies and the men ( it 's almost all men ) who run them love talking up the imminence of AGI because it encourages the rest of us to throw up our hands and abdicate responsibility . <p> " AI systems can recognise patterns , optimise outcomes and simulate human expression , but they do not understand meaning . I 'm sceptical superintelligent machines are the most urgent threat we face . The real risk is that humans abdicate responsibility by treating AI as an autonomous decision-maker . " <p> The label " tech bros " suggests the men who run the big companies are just a bunch of guys goofing around in the lab with some cool stuff . A better generic name for them might be " smart arses " , which describes both their undeniable intelligence and general deplorability . <h> Evil empire ? <p> Author Karen @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ writers festivals , deftly sums up the real risks in the title of her compendious overview of the topic , Empire of AI . " The empire analogy is meant to refer to the fact that tech companies have become the political and economic superpowers of the world , " Hao told the Listener . " They accrue their power through this extractive , exhaustive , exploitative tactic of seizing resources , exhausting labour and taking all the value out of other people , and then not proportionally returning that value to them . <p> " There 's other parallels in the way empires perpetuate their existence through the control of information , and they wrap it around this idea that they 're on this civilising mission to bring progress and modernity to all of humanity . " <p> Dignum points out that AI depends on humans as much as it does on computer power . " There is no AI system that works without people behind the scenes taking care of what 's happening . " She notes that even Waymo self-driving cars in California and other US states @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ just in case . <p> Elsewhere , she notes , badly paid humans mired in cyber cesspits are screening data for unacceptable content , especially sexual or violent material , and often both . " Those people are suffering the psychological and mental problems of dealing with all the dirt that AI can generate based on what we provide to it . " <p> Nor is this safely out of sight and mind in the dusty shacks of the planet 's impoverished population centres : " data scientist " is currently the fourth fastest growing job in the US . <p> More humans will be doing drudge work for a pittance , such as those mired in cyber cesspits screening data for unacceptable content , especially sexual or violent material , and often both . Photo / Getty Images <p> So , more humans doing drudge work for a pittance , more mining precious minerals to make bigger chips , and bigger energy-guzzling data centres anywhere desperate enough to have them . Typical of these is Meta 's Hyperion data centre . To be built in Louisiana , the second @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it will be more than twice the size of Auckland 's CBD and will need a combination of natural gas , nuclear power , solar power and battery storage to feed it . The proposed $5 billion Datagrid AI data centre near Invercargill , at up to 1GW capacity , is a minnow by comparison , but it will have twice the power consumption of the nearby Tiwai Point aluminium smelter . <p> Driving these projects is the tech companies ' belief that progress is a matter of scale : keep making things bigger and eventually the good things -- like AGI -- will magically appear . But the resources that scale is demanding are real and impactful . " The investment that our governments are putting into data centres is investment that they are not putting somewhere else , " says Dignum . <p> OpenAI co-founder Sam Altman has said , " We see a future in which intelligence will be a utility , like electricity or water , and people will buy it from us on a meter . Bostrom theorised it would get to the point where the AI entity would appropriate all resources in its mission and outsmart all efforts to stop it . <p> " The doomsday scenarios are part of the hype , " says Z ? ger in Berlin . " Rich people , as ever throughout history , are out of touch . <p> Alan Blackwell , a New Zealand-born professor of interdisciplinary design at Cambridge University , learnt at Victoria University of Wellington in the 1980s at the feet of Peter Andreae , who 'd recently arrived from MIT 's AI lab . <p> " I think that makes me one of New Zealand 's very first AI graduates , " says Blackwell , author of Moral Codes : Designing Alternatives to AI . The idea that you can make a lot of money and get control over people by telling them the end of the world @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ An AI Treaty ? <p> Assuming , for the sake of argument , that the smart arsey tech bros are not all-powerful , might the best way to control AI actually be to , well , control it ? We have reasonably successful nuclear weapons treaties . We have the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea . We have the Antarctic Treaty System . Traditionally , humans have shown an ability to get along and agree with each other in challenging circumstances . So why not binding international agreements on AI ? <p> Such measures are on the table , says Dignum . " <p> There is no AI system that works without people behind the scenes taking care of what 's happening . <p> Virginia Dignum <p> " What 's needed is a way to totally democratise it and make it a technology that everyone can touch , use , modify , etc , and the way to do that is probably open source , " says Enrique Dans , professor of innovation at the IE University Business School in Madrid . " It 's the approach that China is taking -- the government is trying to force their companies to stay as open source as possible . <p> " If we go open source , you should be able to ... build your own assistant rather than being restricted to whatever some big company wants you to do , and you develop your own additional brain , so to speak . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Organisations now refusing to use AI-generated material include the scientific paper preprint service arXiv.org , Wikipedia , The Guardian and The New York Times . Even Spotify is promising to label AI content as such . <h> No ' I ' in chatbots <p> Like everything else in your computer , AI is a series of 1s and 0s arranged -- coded -- in different ways . When a chatbot says , " Hi , how can I help you with your query today ? " there is no " I " there . It may sound trivial , but many of the humans spoken to for this story believe abstaining from the use of first-person pronouns for AI would make a difference . A chatbot can still do its job without the chitty-chat . <p> Even AI-criticism is anthropomorphic . Bad AI is said to " hallucinate " . But only conscious , sentient entities can hallucinate . Collections of integers can not and do not . They can be badly programmed by humans . <p> At its worst , anthropomorphism sees people developing an addictive , toxic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has led to deaths by suicide . " There 's a legal nonprofit in the US called the Tech Justice Law Project , " says Hao . " They have been leading on a series of lawsuits filed against Open AI for the psychological harms of their products , and in every single one of the lawsuits , they name the anthropomorphism as a key aspect that perpetuates the psychological harm . They are working now with regulators in the US to try to draft bills that would significantly constrain the company 's ability to use anthropomorphism as a tactic . " <p> This is not to discount the good that chatbots and other AI tech can do . ( See " AI for good " , opposite . ) <p> " When I critique certain types of AI technologies , " says Hao , " I 'm not critiquing all AI technologies . I 'm actually very bullish on other types of AI technologies . What I 'm critiquing is the way that these empires insist on developing their technologies through this extraordinarily exploitative and destructive path . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water and chemicals that you are , do to counter the threat of the machine and get in ahead of the terminator and its paperclips ? <p> " It 's very important to have a minimum understanding of the technology outside of the hype , " says Dignum . " And demand our governments keep us informed , keep us safe and keep themselves accountable . Ask ourselves why are we using it ? Why is my government using it ? " <p> As Listener technology columnist Peter Griffin has written ( " Agents of Illusion " , June 6 ) , Finance Minister Nicola Willis 's fever dream that AI can automate public sector tasks and reduce the need for human bureaucrats badly misreads where the technology is at . <h> Resistance not futile <p> Hao sees a precedent in another imperial industry that ravaged the environment and exploited labour en masse : fashion . The solution was not to ban clothes but to use consumer pressure to encourage sustainable and fair practices . <p> Z ? ger has some ambitious strategies . " First is decrease our dependencies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We are in a place where we can make different choices about how to use this technology . AI is often used without a vision of what it is used for . " <p> The hype , and the tendency to think of AI as a superpower issue , obscures alternatives . <p> " There are possibilities of structures that depend more on solidarity , sharing and collaboration rather than private ownership , " Z ? ger says . <p> " For Europe , digital sovereignty is an important topic right now . More and more organisations are thinking , how can we create infrastructures that are less dependent on big tech companies ? <p> " That 's not easy , because they 're very good at their game . " But it could be worth the effort , in case " they ever decide to turn them off , or connect them to even more problematic practices " . <p> The real risk is that humans abdicate responsibility by treating AI as an autonomous decision-maker . <p> Virginia Dignum <p> Hao , together with scholars , journalists , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ AI Resist List , a catalogue of strategies for countering the worst of AI , from language choices to physical actions . Through the Washington-based Pulitzer Center , she also trains journalists in responsible reporting on AI . <p> " Hundreds of communities around the world are protesting against the data centres that the empire needs to train their models , " says Hao . " In 2025 , over $100b of data centre projects were stalled . That has a direct hit on these companies . " <p> The risk in leaving it to politicians is that " a lot of governments are still really enamoured of this idea from the industry that allowing these companies to come is going to boost the economy and bring broad-based productivity to the country . Call your local MP and pressure them to govern the data centres in your communities . There are many popping up in New Zealand . Ask how this data centre is going to affect the resources of your community . " <p> Such grassroots strategies have to overcome a defeatist attitude underlying much of the AI discussion @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rich , everyone else is too smart . University teachers are beaten down by having to mark assignments written by AI . Cheating ? " It 's become so normalised that they do n't see it as an issue , " says one . " Why would they bother doing it the hard way when nobody else is ? " <p> But once you start looking at how we can accommodate AI , deal to its threats and leave room for its benefits , other things fall into place . As Dignum says , " Our most pressing challenges -- climate change , inequality , democratic erosion -- are not problems of insufficient intelligence . They are problems of governance , coordination and collective will . " <p> AI does not determine our fate : we do . In the words of veteran Oxford University AI researcher Michael Wooldridge , " For the foreseeable future , it is a safe bet that far more people will die from natural stupidity than from artificial intelligence . " <h> Te reo ally <p> Peter-Lucas Jones , chief executive of Far North-based broadcaster @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , are singled out by American journalist Karen Hao , in her book Empire of AI , as a dream-case scenario for how AI can be used for a worthwhile purpose , sustainably and ethically . All from Kaitaia , with a fraction of the financial and computing resources of the big AI players , and better results . <p> Te Hiku uses AI-trained language models to help revitalise te reo Maori . " I teach computers how to speak Maori in an effort to reverse language loss and reverse cultural decline so we can increase the number of tools that not only learners , but teachers have , " says Jones . " We 've developed a speech-to-text technology that has an accuracy level of 92% , which is unheard of . " OpenAI , by comparison , signs off on a 50% error rate as acceptable for its speech-to-text technology . <p> Te Hiku 's language models began with its huge archive of cassette tapes in te reo Maori , drawn from 21 radio stations and television channels . Right at the start , Jones and Mahelona sought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " One of the reasons we achieved such great results was because we had a community of passionate supporters that are linked with our tribal radio station , " says Jones . " That is a data pipeline in and out of our language communities . " <p> The enterprise is full of potential and promise . " I 've always taken an affirmative action approach to building capability and capacity , because this journey is n't just about creating language models that can enhance the way we work . It 's also about creating high-value jobs and growing Maori and Pacific Island people into an industry that is largely dominated by Pakeha men in New Zealand and throughout the world . " <p> Award-winning creative agency Motion Sickness is " a company that works to solve problems with creativity for anyone from Whanau Ora to Griffin 's biscuits , " says executive creative director Sam Stuchbury . <p> But it has drawn a line in the sand around AI . " Our belief is that the best ideas start with a human being and end with a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trend . " In our industry , a lot of people are investing in AI and using it for production and saying it 's a key part of our offering . That 's not something we 're really interested in . " <p> He says Motion Sickness has been hiring humans rather than investing in technology . " A lot of people are having the wrong conversation ; it 's not if AI is good or bad , it 's whether you have something worth saying and listening to ? You can still have someone using AI having bad taste and having a bad idea and pushing that out . " <p> Not that the Motion Sickness team are dewy-headed nostalgists . They use AI for " administrative tasks , proofing , stuff like that . But for the stuff that really matters -- a style of writing , or how to come up with an idea , or how to craft an image -- that , to me , needs a human hand . " <h> AI for good <p> Despite its broader promises , AI has n't got @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . But it has done considerable good -- mostly , though not entirely , away from the clutches of Big Tech . A chatbot for women in Mauritania to talk about their experiences of genital mutilation that trauma and shame inhibit them from discussing with a human . -- Virginia Dignum <p> ? Medical imaging that can be used by trained radiologists in early detection of cancer and other diseases . -- Karen Hao 
@@138312152 <h> PM Christopher Luxon 's public sector targets : The hits and misses in latest update <p> Four of the government 's nine key targets risk not being met , the latest update shows . <p> The number of people receiving Jobseeker benefits remains significantly higher than the target of 140,000 and education targets remain elusive . <p> Two health targets are considered " feasible " , but still a way off . <p> A target to reduce the number of households in emergency accommodation has been met well ahead of schedule , as has a goal to reduce the number of victims of assault or robbery . <p> RNZ is tracking progress towards each of the government 's nine targets , which were announced by Prime Minister Christopher Luxon in April 2024 . The targets were chosen to focus the public sector on priorities , and progress is updated each quarter . <p> Each responsible agency assigns a status to progress , ranging from " on track " to " unachievable " . Progress toward reaching a target can still be classed as " feasible " , even if @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as long as the agency in charge believes these can be resolved . <p> The nine targets are set to be delivered by 2030 , beyond the current political term . <h> Health <p> Achieving the goal for 95 percent of patients to be admitted , discharged or transferred from an emergency department within six hours remains far adrift . <p> The latest update shows 74.2 percent of patients are seen within that timeframe - an increase from 68.9 percent in the last quarter . <p> When setting this target , officials warned there was a risk it would not be achievable in the short term . <p> " Most ED emergency departments nationwide are over capacity most of the time , " a briefing to ministers read . <p> This update noted improvements across the whole care system are needed to reduce emergency department wait times . <p> In March , $25 million was announced to tackle winter demand . This includes up to 378 additional staff across the country and 71 extra winter hospital beds . <p> Reaching the 95 percent goal by 2030 is considered feasible , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ second health target for 95 percent of people to receive elective treatment within four months is a long way away from being achieved . <p> At the moment , 64.5 percent of people needing elective treatments , such as hip or cataract surgeries , are seen within four months . This is slightly lower than the 65.9 percent reported in the last quarter . <p> The private sector is being used to tackle the waitlist , with 21,000 procedures to be funded through an Elective Boost programme to June 2026 . <p> Delivery of this target is considered feasible , indicating there are still major risks . <h> Crime <p> The number of serious and persistent youth offenders has decreased from 856 in the last update to 829 and has now reached the target of 900 or fewer well ahead of 2030 . <p> For a youth offender to be classed as a serious or persistent offender they must have committed three or more offences in the past 12 months , with at least one of them having a maximum penalty of seven years ' imprisonment or more . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ school attendance are listed as areas the government is focused on to reach the target . <p> A goal to reduce the number of victims of assault , robbery or sexual assault to 165,000 is classed as on track and has been reached ahead of the 2030 deadline . In this update the total is up by 3000 from the previous quarter 's results , but remains below the target of 165,000 . <p> The goal is based on data from the New Zealand Crime and Victims ' Survey . Officials warned the survey data had a high margin of error and was more suitable for showing long-term trends , with quarterly data described as volatile and likely to fluctuate . <p> The survey asks respondents about their experience of reported and unreported crime over the past 12 months . <p> Initiatives to reach the target include the Three Strikes sentencing reform , gang harm legislation , and new stalking and harassment laws . <h> Employment <p> The number of people receiving Jobseeker support has fallen to 215,200 since the December report , down by 8300 . The update notes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ typical . <p> This target remains classified as " at risk " and is still a considerable way from the goal of 140,000 or fewer people receiving support by 2030 . <p> The government 's update suggested the flow of people receiving the Jobseeker benefit will decrease as economic conditions improve . Measures the update mentioned that will be used to decrease the number include a traffic light system , shifting people into work and increased case management work . <p> A test for Jobseeker eligibility for 18 and 19-year-olds was announced in October 2025 , which will mean applicants whose parents have a combined income above $65,529 will be ineligible for the benefit . It 's estimated 4300 young people will be affected by this . It will come into effect in November . <h> Education <p> At 68 percent , Term 1 's attendance rate is well below the target of 80 percent of students present for more than 90 percent of the term . This means students should take no more than five days off a term . <p> Absence is classed as either " justified " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and other reasons which fall under school policy , such as suspensions . Unjustified absences include truancy , or taking holidays in term time . The government target of 80 percent makes no distinction between the two . <p> Attendance management plans have been implemented from 2026 , these are now mandatory . They include attendance targets for schools , and a process to identify and manage absences . <p> Public information campaigns for 2026 aiming to improve attendance include a focus on illness and taking holidays in term time . <p> Reaching the attendance target is deemed " probable " . <p> Results for writing and mathematics show a small improvement in the latest update , but reading fell slightly . All results are well below the 80 percent target . <p> The update says the government is focusing on a clearer curriculum , a better approach to numeracy and literacy and better reporting . <p> This target is considered to be " at risk " of not being met . <h> Housing <p> Driving down the number of households in emergency housing is one target where progress leapt @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ households , the goal was to reduce the number by 75 percent , to 800 or fewer . <p> The current number is 471 , well below the 800 which was the 2030 goal . Although it is higher than December 's total of 444 . <p> Part of the plan to reach the target includes improving access to other forms of housing for emergency housing residents . As of March 2026 , 1101 households ( with 2367 children ) , have been housed in a social housing tenancy . This is an increase of six households and 27 children since December 's report . <h> Climate <p> Ministry for the Environment / MfE projections corrected in January 2026 <p> There are two targets New Zealand has committed to meeting as part of its net zero climate change goal . <p> The first target is for total greenhouse gas emissions between 2022 and 2025 to be no more than 290 megatonnes . The second target is for total emissions to be no more than 305 megatonnes between 2026 and 2030.

@@138312488 <h> EU tax plan catches big New Zealand steel and aluminum exporters <p> Large New Zealand exporters of manufactured aluminium and steel products will be caught up in a planned extension of the European Union 's carbon tax . <p> The EU 's 2023 Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism ( CBAM ) regulations originally covered materials like raw steel and aluminium , though a limited scope on manufactured goods had left room for circumvention of the rules . <p> " For example , non-EU producers exporting aluminium might instead manufacture semi-finished or finished products , like window frames , to avoid paying the CBAM , while EU manufacturers of those same products face higher input costs due to the ETS ( emissions trading system ) and CBAM , " the European Roundtable on Climate Change and Sustainable Transition ( ERCST ) said in a statement . <p> Chapman Tripp partner Nicola Swan said New Zealand exporters of steel and aluminium to the EU had been reporting and paying a carbon tariff since January . <p> " New Zealand 's aluminium exports to the EU have significantly risen , making this a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said the extension of the CBAM regulations would not include New Zealand 's small manufacturers . <p> " It 's manufactured products that have a high , high steel aluminium content , so we 're talking sort of close to 80 percent upwards , " she said , such as heavy machinery , car parts or specialised aluminium products that might be components of larger products . <p> She said all New Zealand exporters should be alert to these kinds of requirements and be ready to meet these requirements . <p> " I think that these measures , they 're not signalling an immediate move at all towards agricultural products , etc , but certainly it 's a direction of travel , and certainly it shows these types of carbon border adjustment mechanisms bedding down and becoming well understood and well expected for some of these big export markets like the EU . " <p> For example , the UK was expected to introduce CBAM-type measures , and was also being considered in Australia and the United States . <p> " That 's the most important thing I think for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ note of , " Swan said . <h> How CBAM works <p> CBAM came into force in 2023 and imposed a cost on embedded emissions in https : **38;436;TOOLONG ... certain carbon-intensive exports to the EU . <p> The Council of the European Union , the primary decision-making body of the EU , had updated the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism ( CBAM ) framework , amid concerns it was disadvantaging European manufacturers . <p> The 2023 regulations covered primary goods like iron and steel , fertilisers , cement , aluminium , electricity , and hydrogen , though had a limited scope on manufactured goods , which left room for circumvention. 
@@138321565 <p> I first met Barry Soper in 1981 . Although a relatively newbie to the Parliamentary press gallery , Barry was a Stentor guarding over his private radio domain . With that gravel and glue voice , he admonished me for putting a media release in the wrong tray . As a newcomer from the Opposition Labour Research Unit I was put in my place . <p> I rose to become Chief Press Secretary and strategist to Prime Minister David Lange , and worked in various roles for three other Labour Prime Ministers . Now I am a humble olive grower . Barry grew in stature , told a lot of news to a lot of people , and is now the author of a bestselling memoir . One Last Question , Prime Minister is his take on those who occupied the Beehive ninth floor over five decades . <p> His sometimes kind , sometimes brash book reads like a Japanese narrative handscroll . His Ema kimono unravels to tell many a tale in chronological order of PMs . Early on , he sets out his ranking of Prime Ministers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not defined , nor the criteria for assessing it . Significant to who ? Significant by achievement ? Significance by repute ? Significance to Barry ? <p> Across the 12 chapters it appears that Barry prefers PMs who are politically savvy , last a while , know policy , get things done , have low national debt , have loyal Cabinets , play the media 's game , enjoy a drink and joke with journalists , travel well , get noticed , and take Barry 's advice . Helen Clark and John Key come out tops . A non-PM , Winston Peters also ranks highly . Making the country a better place for most New Zealanders is hardly mentioned . <p> Without definition or criteria for " significance " , readers need to entrust or question Barry 's judgment . He relies heavily on his experience , relationships and stories to arrive at his assessment of PMs . It makes for an entertaining read but it also raises questions of accuracy , completeness , and interpretation . <p> For example , Barry ranks Robert Muldoon a " larger than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ projects . Muldoon brandished Think Big on the country in the early 1980s without proven business cases and requiring special empowering legislation . For the record , Muldoon behaved like an autocrat and imposed projects some of which failed court scrutiny and incurred massive intergenerational debt . He left the country nearly bankrupt in 1984 . Barry does not mention these facts . <p> In a later chapter , Barry becomes strident and describes Jacinda Ardern as " the weakest of our significant PMs " in major part because her government increased debt , largely due to Covid . He remonstrates that Ardern did not have " the intellectual capacity to understand exactly what she was doing " and was not " suitable or capable of being PM " . An exasperated Barry , in a tirade against Covid lockdowns , accuses Adern 's government of imposing a " totalitarian state " . <p> Two further points on significance and completeness . Historians rate Muldoon 's greatest contribution to New Zealand as signing the Closer Economic Relations free trade agreement with Australia . Lange 's contribution was giving New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Are they significant ? <p> Geoffrey Palmer is rated lowly as a PM . Barry fails to see Palmer as the most recognised legislator and legal reformer of modern New Zealand . On PM Mike Moore , Barry is complimentary , not so much of Moore as PM but his later roles as Ambassador to the US and joint head of the World Trade Organisation . He dismisses Palmer and Moore as insignificant PMs but apart from Helen Clark , few other modern PMs have gone on to achieve such significance . <p> There are factual quibbles . Barry states that Muldoon 's ministerial colleagues let down his car tyres to prevent him driving home inebriated . It was a public servant , private secretary Malcolm Fern , who deflated Muldoon 's tyres . <p> The Lange chapter contains several clangers . For the record , and well documented , deputy PM Geoffrey Palmer wrote the memo that provided the policy position for David Lange to reject the proposed USS Buchanan visit to New Zealand in 1985 . Barry claims " Lange would have allowed nuclear-propelled vessels to visit @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , led by Hamilton 's Margaret Wilson . " Wrong . First , Lange fully endorsed Labour Party policy to ban nuke powered and armed ships in 1983 , not long after Lange became Labour leader . I wrote the statement . No Margaret involved . <p> On the Buchanan invitation , I took Palmer 's memorandum and other material to Lange in Pago Pago , American Samoa , and met him on his return from Tokelau . Lange had not decided on the Buchanan request but on reading Palmer 's memo , and in a very frank discussion with me on the merits of rejecting the request , he made up his mind . No government has dared change that decision and its empowering legislation . PM Luxon is a prime recent example . <p> On India , Barry says Lange never returned to India after meeting Mrs Gandhi , a trip Barry was on . Lange returned a matter of weeks later to attend Mrs Gandhi 's funeral following her assassination , a trip Barry did not go on . <p> Barry seems confused on Lange 's significance @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by the tide , wherever that would take him -- and where it increasingly took him was the world of Rogernomics " . Later , on Lange 's legacy Barry writes : " Lange was the lever puller of the entire operation . He was a populist Prime Minister in that he was extraordinarily well thought of by the public , through sheer force of his personality rather than policy governance . " <p> In fact Cabinet under Lange never took one vote . It was governed by consensus . Further , Barry never mentions Lange 's Tomorrow Schools policy , that lasted nearly five decades . And then there is the matter of Lange finally cementing PM Norman Kirk 's dream of New Zealand being nuclear free and having an independent foreign policy . Both are lasting policy . <p> In the same Lange chapter , Barry attributes to Roger Douglas changes in how press secretaries were and are employed in the Beehive . Roger would be surprised . Journalist David Exel wrote a report on Beehive press secretaries ' employment . That became the basis for the revolution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ employment of press secretaries . That was my doing , and each week I reported to Cabinet on coordinating communications , including media releases and paid advertising . <p> I was the first press secretary employed under that new regime and went on to manage Lange 's historic re-election campaign in 1987 . I was not a member of the Labour Party . <p> Barry naturally uses his experiences for his narrative . But is this sufficient ? For example , Barry decries Lange 's policy credentials and says the Fourth Labour government spent the first term " largely on ideology " , whatever this means . He then goes on to describe the first term and probably the most significant economic and foreign policy changes made by any New Zealand government since Michael Joseph Savage in the 1930s . They have largely lasted . <p> In Barry 's chapter on PM Helen Clark , Barry opines that despite what most economists and historians think , his view is that Clark and finance minister Cullen were greater reformers than the Lange government . They have a proud record but the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's economic and foreign policy settings . They did , however , vastly improve social and economic protections for most New Zealanders . <p> A reader may get the impression that Barry seems more at ease with National PMs . And yet in terms of significance , avuncular Jim Bolger 's contribution is hard to fathom . He was largely defined by his ministers Ruth Richardson , Bill Birch , Winston Peters , Doug Graham , and Jenny Shipley . <p> John Key appeared easy going and popular . The question is how do his achievements stack up against those of PMs Ardern , Clark , Lange and Muldoon ? Key 's achievements seem to be handling the global economic crisis with calmness , taking New Zealand into the Paris Agreement on climate change , accepting the advice of a taxi driver to build cycleways ( the latter two not mentioned by Barry ) and lasting nearly three terms . <p> Knowing what makes a significant PM is important . It 's one of the toughest jobs there is . The ones I have known and worked with are multi-talented @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extraordinary hours away from their families , and are constantly on show , on the go , on guard . <p> Surely , significance should be equivalent to public service contribution to make all New Zealand a better , more prosperous , fairer place to live , work and , to use the term Barry hates , for New Zealanders to enjoy a sense of wellbeing that brings social and economic betterment , hope and happiness . <p> One Last Question , Prime Minister by Barry Soper ( HarperCollins , $39.99 ) is available in bookstores nationwide . <p> Ross Vintiner worked for three Prime Ministers , and is more famous for talking about enriching the soil on his Martinborough grove Dali Olives on an episode of Hyundai Country Calendar . @@138336682 <h> South Dunedin : do nothing not an option <p> ' ' The flat ' ' of Dunedin -- where people want to thrive and live well . PHOTO:ODT FILES <p> Resolving flooding issues in South Dunedin is a health priority , Prof Alex Macmillan and Sarah Harrison write.The health and wellbeing of South Dunedin communities should be at the heart of council decision-making about climate change-related increases in flooding and sea-level rise . <p> We have seen a lot of attention paid so far to pipes , roads and buildings , but this can miss community needs and priorities . Having worked alongside South Dunedin community members , we know that they care deeply about the ability of all in their place to thrive and live well , in a healthy environment . <p> Our Healthy Climate Adaptation in South Dunedin report , released last week , shows that the multiple links between climate change and the building blocks of health play out in many ways over time . The decisions being made about South Dunedin have the opportunity to address health risks , but careful design will be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ report brings together South Dunedin community voices , aspirations and knowledge from our earlier research , with updated health evidence to understand how the three South Dunedin short-listed futures could improve or worsen health and wellbeing . <p> Our team compared the three possible futures with each other , as well as with a ' ' do nothing further ' ' scenario , and found that not progressing any long-term well-planned adaptation pathway would be the worst choice for health , and widen existing health inequalities . <p> Our previous research with South Dunedin community members makes clear that flooding has significant negative effects on people 's wellbeing . <p> As a community member told us : ' ' One of the impacts of flooding is fear and the sense of helplessness , which leads to depression and/or anger ... it sits like a miasma over this stuff ' ' . <p> As well as these effects on mental health , worsening flooding and sea-level rise are already affecting physical health . Community members recognise the importance of these : ' ' Ongoing health , particularly respiratory issues , from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cycle through people missing work , kids miss school and then that starts having other impacts -- it 's a bit of a vicious cycle . ' ' <p> The traumatic nature of floods , financial stress , and the lingering effects of damp , mouldy homes all have flow-on effects for people 's ability to enjoy living in their community , attend school , go to work , socialise with friends , and afford the things they need to live well . <p> As well as showing how to protect people 's health from flooding , we identify opportunities for council to design in healthy communities and a healthy environment for each option , as well as ensuring health harms are n't borne disproportionately by those who have the least resources . <p> For example , the city can make the most of necessary changes by redeveloping neighbourhoods for healthy homes , fair access to daily needs , facilities and services , and maintaining the strong sense of community that already makes South Dunedin special . <p> This includes improved , affordable access to parks , everyday destinations and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bus . Good long-term planning for water means healthy people , as well as healthy streams and harbour ecosystems that can flourish . <p> The need to make way for water means there are some areas where land use will need to change away from homes to floodable areas to keep people safe , while leaving many areas remaining as homes . This will affect the whole city 's housing . <p> Our report makes clear that simply leaving housing and land governance to the market in South Dunedin risks widening housing-related health injustices across the city . <p> Regardless of which future pathway is chosen , how it is implemented matters very much . The report recommends : <p> ( 1 ) supporting people through stress , grief and trauma , by ensuring strong social connections and support services are maintained and improved ; <p> ( 2 ) ensuring decent , affordable , healthy homes for those who have to move , and for those who remain in South Dunedin ; and <p> ( 3 ) designing in opportunities for improving health and reducing health injustices from the start @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health and wellbeing , and fairness , at the heart of decisions on flooding and sea-level rise in South Dunedin . We urge decision-makers to incorporate the report 's recommendations into future design and investment . <p> As one community member said , ' ' If a community feels that it is being listened to and engaged with in real terms , then the ability to be resilient to change ... will rise and it 's going to be a major positive in terms of community welfare . ' ' <p> * Alex Macmillan is a public health physician and professor of environmental public health at the University of Otago and tangata Tiriti co-director of Climate Health Aotearoa national research centre ; Sarah Harrison is a climate change adaptation and public health researcher at Urban Intelligence , and a member of Climate Health Aotearoa.

@@138337013 <h> -- Advertisement -- <p> SB64 ended with a deadlock among Parties in the negotiations regarding the Global Goal on Adaptation . Many developing countries , and in particular the Africa Group of Nations , drew a line in the sand in the negotiations on Adaptation . Their line was crossed when they failed to get agreement from developed countries on the inclusion in the text of the tripling of Adaptation finance , promised at COP30 in Bel ? m . The negotiations on the Global Goal on Adaptation will now have to start from scratch at the COP in Antalya . This outright rejection of the GGA text is a display of the built up frustrations by many developing countries about the complete lack of commitment and bad faith by developed countries to fulfil their legal obligation to provide climate finance . This was a feature that affected all negotiation rooms . <p> What was supposed to mark the transition from commitment to implementation in Bonn instead exposed a negotiation process that increasingly speaks the language of implementation while repeatedly retreating into procedure when implementation requires decisions . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Across two weeks of negotiations , governments repeatedly affirmed the importance of Adaptation , Just Transition , resilience and supporting communities on the frontlines of the climate crisis . Few openly challenged these priorities . The language of a people-centred transition has become mainstream . <h> -- Advertisement -- <p> Yet progress on the provision of real support continued to lag behind progress on process . <p> When discussions turned to the finance , governance arrangements and institutional decisions needed to make those commitments real , negotiations repeatedly drifted back towards reviews , technical processes , terms of reference and procedural debates . <p> The result was a conference in which implementation was universally endorsed but frequently deferred . From Adaptation and Just Transition to mitigation , the central political struggle was no longer over whether action is needed , but over who has the power and the resources to make implementation possible . <p> For millions of people already facing devastating floods , prolonged droughts , extreme heat , food insecurity and displacement , climate change is not a future risk but a present reality . Communities can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on declarations . Resilience can not be built through reviews and technical discussions . The test at COP31 will be whether governments are prepared to match their words with the finance , support and political action needed to turn commitments into reality . <p> Jacobo Ocharan , Head of Political Strategies at CAN International , said : " Governments arrived in Bonn talking about implementation , but too often they killed time by talking about process . The debate over climate action has changed . The debate over who pays for it , governs it and delivers it has not . Across Adaptation , Just Transition , mitigation and finance , progress slowed whenever negotiations reached the decisions needed to turn commitments into reality . Communities facing climate impacts today do not need another review , another dialogue or another workshop about implementation . They need governments to deliver on what they have already promised . " <p> The rejection of the Global Goal on Adaptation text came down to the lack of commitment by developed countries to fulfil their legal obligation to not only provide , but triple finance @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Bonn session largely steered away from finance and means of implementation , focusing instead on technical processes and indicators and not the means to implement them . <p> Pooja Dave , Adaptation Policy Coordinator at Climate Action Network International , said : " What we saw in Bonn was clear bad faith and unwillingness by developed countries to make progress on the most important issue on Adaptation : the Global Goal on Adaptation . You can not implement the GGA without finance . Yet continued attention was given to the technical processes , while progress on Adaptation finance remained limited . Developed countries largely focused discussions on the Technical Task Force , while developing countries stressed that all GGA mandates matter -- including the commitment to triple Adaptation finance . For communities already living through floods , droughts and extreme heat , implementation is not about measuring Adaptation . It is about making it possible . Countries on the frontlines already know what they need to do . The question is whether they will receive the finance and support needed to do it . " <p> Unlike the Adaptation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ negotiations produced a text outlining functions and modalities for a future Just Transition Mechanism . The emerging Bel ? m-to-Antalya Mechanism ( BAM ) is intended to turn the political commitments agreed at COP30 into practical support for workers , communities and countries navigating the transition . The text represents an important step towards operationalising that vision . However , key questions around governance , accountability , participation and implementation support remain unresolved . Civil society organisations repeatedly warned against allowing procedural debates and reviews to crowd out the substantive work needed to build a mechanism capable of identifying implementation gaps , mobilising support and helping deliver just transitions on the ground . <p> Anabella Rosemberg , Senior Advisor on Just Transition at CAN International , said : " The Bel ? m-to-Antalya Mechanism for Just Transition ( BAM ) is now within reach . Bonn was often slow and frustrating , but it produced the foundations for a meaningful outcome at COP31 . The next three months will determine whether governments build a mechanism that supports workers , communities and countries through the transition , or settle for another @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , mobilise support and strengthen cooperation on just transition . CAN and its allies will continue pushing for a mechanism that moves beyond discussion and helps make just transition a reality . " <p> Finance remained the unresolved political question beneath almost every negotiation at SB64 . Whether the issue was adaptation , Just Transition , mitigation or loss and damage , developing countries repeatedly stressed that implementation depends on finance , technology transfer and capacity-building reaching the countries and communities expected to deliver climate action . Yet most developed countries remain unwilling to commit the scale of public , grant-based finance needed to match their rhetoric on implementation . Looking away does not make the need disappear . It simply leaves vulnerable countries and communities to carry the burden and the costs , including paying the price with lives lost . <p> The transition away from fossil fuels remained another test of whether governments are prepared to move from agreement to implementation . While Parties continue to endorse the commitment agreed at COP28 , Bonn offered limited clarity on how the transition is being delivered in practice , particularly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with no real support to do this . <p> Mitigation negotiations reflected the same pattern . While Parties increasingly embraced the language of implementation , discussions continued to avoid the harder questions of ambition , support and how to close the emissions gap at the speed required by science . The risk is that implementation becomes another agenda item to discuss rather than a mandate to deliver . <p> As governments now turn towards COP31 in Antalya , the challenge is no longer defining the purpose of climate action . The science is clear , and what we need now is the strengthening of the IPCC , the backbone of the UNFCCC process , especially as it brings in more scientific voices from the Global South , as well as local and Indigenous knowledge systems . There is broad agreement , grounded in science and lived reality , that climate action must protect people , strengthen resilience , advance equity and support a Just Transition . The question is whether governments are prepared to move beyond process and take the decisions needed to deliver it .

" <h> Building One of New Zealand 's Largest Dairy Businesses <p> Dairy Holdings now holds around 90 farms , both dairy and support , producing around 33 million kgMS annually . <p> Under Glass 's leadership the company continued to grow right to the end of his tenure , with the acquisition @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which only went unconditional on his last day at work , and was settled on the Wednesday of the following week . <p> With Rangitata holding several dairy farms in South Canterbury and a large grazing block in Tarras , Glass said it was " quite a large transaction and good one to get under our belt at the end " . <p> Glass said the success of the company was the testament to the vision and courage of the founders , Allan Hubbard , Colin and Dale Armer , and Alan Pye , to pick up the farms of the former Tasman Agriculture and Dairy Brands groups when their then owners decided to sell up in 2001 . <p> As Tasman Ag 's chief financial officer at the time , Glass came with the deal and he proceeded to lead the new company through an extraordinary growth period for dairying . <h> Overcoming Early Challenges <p> Glass said many of the challenges of the early years were the sometimes very involved legal processes around water take consents that had to be pursued through various regional council hearings and in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " Out of all of that , there were some amazing learnings and that really has set Dairy Holdings up for the future . <p> " Most of those 70-odd dairy farms in Canterbury now are all incredibly well irrigated with reliable , relatively low-cost , and affordable water . So it 's meant that the overall portfolio is now really , really resilient and able to understand most price shocks . <p> " The piece for me that was quite nice , was how the team and everyone involved at that time was able to take some of those challenges and knockbacks and learn from them , and it was out of that that the company really grew . <p> " When you look back now , any difficult times we experience is always what makes you stronger . Never have you finished your learning and if you just stay focused , you can always get there in the end . " <p> Dairy remained the absolute cornerstone for rural New Zealand and so many businesses livelihoods were dependent on it , he says . <p> " Thank goodness that we do have dairy because if we did n't , you know there would n't be too much really holding up the New Zealand economy at the moment . " <p> " It 's just dreamland really . I thought it was a spoof when I first read it and was wondering who 'd put it out , but it turns out maybe they 're serious . " <p> One of the features of the report is a world where developed nations would recognise when they had enough stuff , stop growing , and stop producing goods we do n't really need . @@138354871 <h> #national : Disaster Laws Leaving Whanau At Risk , New Research Warns <p> A new doctoral study has found New Zealand 's disaster laws are failing to adequately protect the right to housing , leaving communities vulnerable to repeating many of the same mistakes exposed following the Canterbury earthquakes . Research by Dr Maude Loutsch argues that while emergency response systems have evolved since the devastating earthquakes of 2010 and 2011 , ... <p> A new doctoral study has found New Zealand 's disaster laws are failing to adequately protect the right to housing , leaving communities vulnerable to repeating many of the same mistakes exposed following the Canterbury earthquakes . <p> Research by Dr Maude Loutsch argues that while emergency response systems have evolved since the devastating earthquakes of 2010 and 2011 , significant legal gaps remain that could leave thousands of people without adequate housing protections during future disasters . <p> The findings come as climate change increases the frequency and severity of floods , storms , coastal erosion and other natural hazards across Aotearoa . <p> Dr Loutsch says one of the most significant weaknesses in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recognising housing as a fundamental human right during and after disasters . <p> The Canterbury earthquakes highlighted how difficult recovery can become when people lose access to secure housing and face uncertainty about rebuilding , relocation and long-term support . <p> While emergency powers often focus on infrastructure , public safety and recovery management , the research suggests housing rights can become secondary considerations despite being central to individual and community wellbeing . <p> Low-income households , renters , Maori communities , disabled people and those living in vulnerable housing situations are often the least equipped to absorb the impacts of a disaster and can face prolonged displacement . <p> For many whanau , losing a home can trigger a cascade of challenges including financial hardship , disruption to education , reduced access to healthcare and the loss of important social and cultural connections . <p> The research also points to growing concerns around climate adaptation and managed retreat policies , where communities may need to relocate due to repeated flooding , coastal erosion or other environmental threats . <p> Dr Loutsch 's work calls for housing rights to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Among the reforms proposed are stronger legal obligations on government agencies to ensure access to safe and secure housing following disasters , improved accountability mechanisms and clearer frameworks for supporting communities facing relocation . <p> The study also highlights the need for disaster planning to move beyond emergency response and place greater emphasis on prevention , preparedness and resilience . <p> As climate-related risks continue to increase across New Zealand , the research argues that governments can no longer treat housing as an afterthought in disaster management . <p> Instead , housing security should be recognised as a cornerstone of recovery and community resilience . <p> The findings arrive as councils , iwi , government agencies and communities grapple with how to respond to growing environmental threats and the realities of climate change . <p> For Maori communities in particular , the protection of housing is closely linked to whakapapa , whenua and the ability of whanau to remain connected to their communities and cultural identity . <p> The research concludes that without meaningful legal reform , New Zealand risks repeating many of the housing failures experienced after Canterbury @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> With severe weather events becoming more common , the study serves as a warning that the next major disaster may not be a question of if , but when .

@@138462511 <h> Rules-based order ' more important ' than ever <p> The co-ordinator of the 60th Otago Foreign Policy School says the ' ' rules ' ' New Zealand once understood are more ' ' at threat ' ' and yet ' ' more important ' ' than in decades . <p> Prof Robert Patman opened proceedings for the weekend symposium held at the University of Otago 's St Margaret 's College last night . <p> He said the rules-based international order , which New Zealand had been one of the strongest proponents of , was starting to fray under a ' ' multitude of events ' ' . <p> These ranged from the US invasion of Iraq in 2003 , to Russia 's invasion of Ukraine in 2022 and the US-Israeli war on Iran . <p> ' ' I think to some extent , our sense of identity at home is linked to our belief that the international arena should also be characterised by rules and that has been the traditional , that has been the post-1945 approach of all governments irrespective of whether they 're Labour or National-led . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is that we think New Zealand is facing one of the most challenging international environments but it 's important , first of all , to have a discussion , a debate about these issues in New Zealand so that we can make informed choices . ' ' <p> Prof Patman said many of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of both alarm and hope ' ' . <p> ' ' Many of the key problems faced by the world , whether they be climate change , pandemics , transnational terrorism , or even economic contagion , the sort we 've seen from the Middle East -- when the United States attacked Iran , Iran retaliated -- have had cascading effects for the global economy . <p> ' ' The Ukraine conflict has had the same impact as well . <p> ' ' In other words , the world is seeing a proliferation of trans-boundary problems , which do n't respect borders .
